## 1. [EDGE-INFERNO: The chemical signatures of assembly history in reionization fossils](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18473) <a id="link1"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18473

**Authors:** Eric P. Andersson, Martin P. Rey, Alexander P. Ji, Justin I. Read, Oscar Agertz, Alexander H. Riley, Stacy Y. Kim, Astha Chaturvedi, Jennifer Mead, Kaley Brauer, Mordecai-Mark Mac Low, Andrew Pontzen

**Abstract:** In galaxies, stellar populations represent a superposition of both in situ and accreted components, tracing internal chemical evolution as well as the histories of merged satellites. We investigate how assembly history shapes the chemical properties of present-day stellar populations in low-mass dwarf galaxies. We analyze a suite of EDGE-INFERNO star-by-star cosmological zoom-in simulations of galaxies with present-day halo mass $\sim1.4\times10^9\,{\rm M}_{\odot}$. We systematically vary galaxy growth while preserving the present-day halo mass, engineering our simulations to span a continuum of assembly histories. Early-forming systems form most of their stars in a single progenitor, with centrally concentrated star formation and a continuous enrichment history. In contrast, our late-forming systems assemble by accretion of multiple progenitors with their own stellar populations, resulting in fragmented star formation across several halos with independent chemical evolution tracks. These regimes produce qualitatively different chemical signatures: single-progenitor systems exhibit connected, well-defined trends in abundance space, while multi-progenitor systems show multiple, distinct stellar populations with limited overlap. We find that metallicity distribution functions provide initial constraints on assembly history for samples of at least $50$-$100$ member stars. Multi-element abundance analysis significantly improves the recovery of distinct components, although robust reconstruction still requires larger samples. Fully separating in situ and accreted populations likely requires kinematic information. Accreted stars can contribute substantially even at high metallicities in multi-progenitor systems, while external enrichment can cause accreted material to follow similar chemical trends as in situ stars, particularly in the single-progenitor regime.

**Comment:** Dwarf galaxy simulations, assembly history, near-field cosmology

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

## 2. [MUSE-DARK IV. Comparing Dark Matter Densities to Star Formation Histories in 375 intermediate-z galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18282) <a id="link2"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18282

**Authors:** B. I. Ciocan, N. F. Bouché, J. Fensch, D. Krajnović, J. Freundlich, H. Desmond, A. Jeanneau, B. Famaey

**Abstract:** (Abridged) Baryonic feedback is expected to transform cuspy dark-matter (DM) haloes into cores, but direct observational links between inner DM slopes and galaxy star-formation histories (SFHs) remain limited. We investigate and quantify the connection between halo inner slopes and baryonic assembly histories in a large sample of intermediate-z galaxies. We analyse 375 galaxies at 0.1<z<1.43 spanning 7<log(Mstar/Msun)<11.2 using 3D forward modelling of MUSE kinematics and disk-halo decompositions with mass-dependent (DC14-based) and generalised Navarro-Frenk-White (gNFW) halo profiles. Stellar population parameters are inferred from multi-band Spectral Energy Distribution fitting of UV-to-MIR photometry using a non-parametric SFH model. Bayesian model comparison shows that the DC14-based halo density profiles are statistically preferred over gNFW for a substantial fraction of the sample. The inferred inner slopes from gNFW fits nevertheless follow the DC14 expectation and reproduce the expected dependence on the stellar-to-halo mass ratio, despite this not being explicitly imposed. Partial correlation coefficient and random forest regression analyses consistently identify stellar mass (or, equivalently, the stellar-to-halo mass ratio) as the primary driver of the inner density slope, a result that holds robustly even when the analysis is restricted to the gNFW fits. In contrast, SFH-related quantities (e.g. formation time and 'burstiness' indicators) exhibit no independent predictive power on the DM halo inner slope once the dependence on stellar mass is accounted for. Cored systems preferentially occupy lower-mass, more bursty bins with later assembly times, whereas cuspy systems are associated with higher stellar masses, lower star formation activity, and earlier assembly epochs, in agreement with theoretical expectations of stellar-feedback-induced cores.

**Comment:** Dark matter core-cusp vs star formation histories

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 3. [Atomic hydrogen in $z=0$ galaxies in the COLIBRE simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18046) <a id="link3"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18046

**Authors:** Kyle A. Oman, Ruby J. Wright, Andrea Gebek, Diego Dado, Katherine E. Harborne, Evgenii Chaikin, Carlos S. Frenk, Filip Huško, Claudia D. P. Lagos, Aaron D. Ludlow, Sylvia Ploeckinger, Alexander J. Richings, Matthieu Schaller, Joop Schaye, James W. Trayford

**Abstract:** We present an overview of the properties of atomic hydrogen (HI) in galaxies at redshift $z=0$ in the COLIBRE cosmological galaxy formation simulations. COLIBRE directly simulates the multi-phase interstellar medium (ISM) and includes new methods to follow radiative cooling to $\approx 10\,\rm{K}$, non-equilibrium chemistry coupled to dust, and dust formation, growth \& destruction. We focus on the $(200\,\rm{cMpc})^3$ volume with resolution elements of $2\times 10^{6}\,\rm{M}_{\odot}$. No HI properties informed the calibration of model parameters and yet we find very good agreement with the observed $M_{\star}$-$M_{\rm{HI}}$ relation, the HI mass and velocity width functions, the HI mass-size relation, and (baryonic) Tully-Fisher relations, albeit with some mismatches at the extremes of the galaxy population. HI surface density profiles are realistic, except at large radii around massive ($M_{\star}\gtrsim 10^{10}\,\rm{M}_{\odot}$) galaxies where we find a population of possibly spurious circumgalactic HI clouds, whose abundances and masses decrease, however, with increasing mass resolution). Using smaller simulations to vary some of the uncertain model parameters, we find that the main scaling relations are at most mildly affected, although this may not extend to detailed HI morphokinematics. Convergence across mass resolution levels a factor of $8$ higher and lower is good: the median HI mass and HI `occupation fraction' as a function of halo mass, and the HI mass function, are remarkably stable, although the highest resolution model overproduces HI sources by a factor of $\approx2$ at $M_{\rm{HI}}\gtrsim 3\times 10^{9}\,\rm{M}_{\odot}$. Upturns in the low-mass and low-velocity slopes of the HI mass and velocity width functions with potentially subtle observational signatures are predicted.

**Comment:** High-resolution galaxy simulations with new ISM/chemistry physics

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 4. [Environmental Dependence of Satellite Planarity and Orbital-Pole Coherence around Milky Way Analogues in a Hydrodynamical Cosmological Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16710) <a id="link4"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16710

**Authors:** Peng Wang, Yu Luo, Jiang Chang, Xi Kang, Shi Shao

**Abstract:** We examine whether the unusual spatial flattening and orbital-plane coherence of the Milky Way's classical satellites are connected to the Galaxy's location in the cosmic web. We compare the 11 classical satellites with the 11 most massive satellites of 11,922 Milky Way-mass hosts in the TNG300-1 hydrodynamical simulation. We measure flattening with both the simple inertia tensor (SIT), which retains radial information, and the reduced inertia tensor (RIT), which isolates angular anisotropy. Orbital-plane coherence is characterized by the minimum opening angle containing the axes of the eight most coplanar satellite orbits. For the Milky Way, we find $(c/a,b/a)=(0.183,0.558)$ for SIT and $(0.364,0.736)$ for RIT, together with an eight-orbit opening angle of $21.9^\circ$. The corresponding fractions of simulated systems at least as extreme are $4.94\%$, $12.28\%$, and $5.04\%$, respectively. We describe the environment using the velocity-shear tensor smoothed on $2\,h^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}$ and compare the full sample with Local Group-like pairs, systems whose shear eigenvalues match those reconstructed for the Local Group, and hosts in knots, filaments, sheets, and voids. Among the well-sampled environmental subsets, the shape and opening-angle distributions differ only weakly. Although the cosmic web imprints preferred orientations on host spins and satellite orbital-plane axes, the Milky Way combines orientations that are uncommon in the simulation. We conclude that the present-day cosmic-web environment alone does not substantially increase the occurrence of Milky Way-like satellite configurations. This result does not rule out an origin in anisotropic accretion, since neither the present-day web class nor its shear eigenvalues fully encodes the assembly history of an individual satellite system.

**Comment:** Milky Way satellite planarity and orbital coherence in TNG

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 5. [Magnetic field evolution in feedback-dominated galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17216) <a id="link5"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17216

**Authors:** Yue Samuel Lu, Dušan Kereš, Philip F. Hopkins, Sam B. Ponnada

**Abstract:** Over the last decade, magnetic fields (B-fields) have become a common ingredient in galaxy formation simulations. Although seemingly unimportant in matching observational constraints such as the $M_*-M_{\rm halo}$ relations, the B-field influences the circumgalactic medium (CGM) gas and determines cosmic ray (CR) propagation, making it an indispensable non-thermal component. Nevertheless, the intricate physics of B-fields, particularly how they evolve to the observed $μ$G-level field, remains largely elusive. In this work, we systematically address the evolution of B-fields over cosmic time using the FIRE-2 (Feedback In Realistic Environments) magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) simulations. We present results for both Milky Way (MW)-mass and dwarf galaxies, with and without CRs. We find that CRs have little impact on B-field properties, while the halo mass controls the saturated B-field strength at $z\sim 0$. However, all runs reach a halo-independent magnetic-to-turbulent ratio of $f_{\rm B}\sim 1-10\%$, though the methods used to measure turbulence could affect this value. We identify a three-stage evolutionary course for the B-field in tandem with galaxy formation: i. expansion, ii. amplification, and iii. saturation. The feature is universal, though the timings depend on the initial location and star formation history of the gas. Flux-freezing is the predominant driver for the first and last stages, exhibiting power relations $B\sim ρ^{2/3}$ and $ρ^{1/2}$, although the latter could also be explained by dynamical and energetic equilibria. The intermediate amplification stage, potentially driven by ``dynamos'', is fuelled by structure formation, feedback, and gas turbulence. We conjecture a numerically triggered, turbulence-driven amplification process to explain this phenomenon.

**Comment:** Galaxy simulations with new physics, dwarf galaxies, CGM

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 6. [Self-Interacting Dark Matter: From Particle Physics Theories to Structure Formation Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16968) <a id="link6"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16968

**Authors:** Martin Rosenlyst, Vinh Tran, Oliver Zier, Xuejian Shen, Mark Vogelsberger, Thejs Brinckmann, Mads T. Frandsen, Jesús Zavala, Frederik Holt Dihver Christensen, Mikkel Theiss Kristensen

**Abstract:** We establish a unified theory-to-simulation framework for self-interacting dark matter (SIDM) that integrates three components: (i) a comprehensive, self-contained treatment of its particle-physics foundations and a broad range of model classes; (ii) a systematic, quantitative mapping from microscopic dark-sector physics to structure-formation simulation inputs through explicit expressions and numerical prescriptions; and (iii) the development, implementation, and validation of a broad suite of SIDM algorithms and a dedicated DM decay module in the moving-mesh code AREPO-2. Under (i), we detail the particle content, symmetries, interactions, production mechanisms, and constraints of a broad landscape of elementary and composite SIDM models, and present representative viable benchmark models. Under (ii), the mapping specifies cosmological initial conditions, internal-state masses and populations, reaction networks, velocity- and angle-dependent cross sections, dissipative energy loss, and decay rates and recoil kinematics. We then apply this mapping to a broad range of SIDM model classes. Under (iii), we implement and validate SIDM algorithms with constant and velocity-dependent cross sections, covering isotropic and anisotropic scattering, multi-state elastic and inelastic reactions, and dissipative processes, alongside a dedicated DM decay module in AREPO-2. We validate these implementations through beam-lattice deflection experiments, thermalization and uniform-box decay tests, isolated-halo gravothermal core-collapse simulations, and cosmological box and zoom-in simulations. These tests recover analytical predictions, establish agreement across numerical schemes, and reproduce published benchmarks. Our theory-to-simulation framework serves as a cross-disciplinary reference and a practical bridge between SIDM particle physics and simulations of nonlinear structure formation.

**Comment:** SIDM simulations, core-cusp and substructure relevance

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 7. [Identifying the principal mechanism of star formation feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16557) <a id="link7"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16557

**Authors:** Chong Li, Rouyu Li, Keping Qiu, Marko Krco, Di Li, Hongchi Wang, Xin Lyu, Yan Duan, Min Fang, Xuepeng Chen, Lile Wang, Haoran Feng, Shuinai Zhang, Miaomiao Zhang, Shaobo Zhang, Yue Cao, Yao Wang, Yan Gong, Fujun Du, Zhiwei Chen, Yang Su, Yuehui Ma, Yuchen Xing, Yinghui Zheng, Tianwei Zhang, Shu Niu, Chenrui Li, Jiawei Liu, Zhijun Zhang, Yulian Guo

**Abstract:** Feedback from massive stars regulates the formation of next-generation stars and planets and thus shapes the evolution of galaxies, yet observational evidence capable of determining which mechanism dominates the feedback process has long been lacking. Here,we present a large-scale HI survey toward Orion A$.$ With an unprecedented sensitivity and superior resolution, FAST (Five-hundred-meter Aperture Spherical radio Telescope) reveals a well-defined heart-shaped expanding HI bubble, enshrouding the well-known "Veil" bubble of ionized gas. The photoionization-driven and wind-driven gas expands at 10.9 and 13.8 kilometers per second, respectively, with a kinetic energy ratio of 1:2 assuming a spherical symmetry and a uniform HI-to-H2 ratio. This first direct comparison within a single bubble shows that radiation does not dominate the feedback process as predicted by classical theories; instead, stellar winds contribute a comparable share of the injected kinetic energy.

**Comment:** Star formation feedback, wind-driven outflows in Orion

## 8. [Untangling Similarities and Differences in Star Formation Histories Across Cosmological Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17359) <a id="link8"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17359

**Authors:** Alicia M. Savelli, Matthew A. Kustec, Joshua S. Speagle, Kartheik G. Iyer, Eshal Ahmed, Zehao Peng, Norman Murray, Cissy Kuang

**Abstract:** How galaxies assemble depends on processes ranging from small-scale baryonic physics to large-scale cosmological assembly, which are difficult to disentangle with observations alone. Using $\sim$63,000 simulated galaxies from seven cosmological simulations spanning hydrodynamical, semi-analytic, and semi-empirical models, we explore how specific star formation histories (sSFHs) vary across modelling assumptions. Using non-linear dimensionality reduction (UMAP), we find that sSFH shape variation organizes along a few smooth axes in a 2D embedding, broadly corresponding to (1) peak timing, (2) peak sharpness, and (3) subsequent quenching rate. We identify sSFH shapes that many simulations share ("common"), well described to first order by dark matter halo assembly, and a few tied to individual simulations ("unique"), which likely reflect subgrid and parametrization choices such as merger-triggered burst prescriptions. A notable exception is a common population of rapidly quenched galaxies found across most simulations, likely driven by e.g., active galactic nucleus (AGN) feedback, that halo assembly alone cannot reproduce. The origin of the most distinctive unique shape, an Illustris-dominated history with an early peak and slow decline, remains unclear, but we test the effects of short-term variability. With a multi-objective auto-encoder neural network, we recover the simulation that a given sSFH is drawn from with $\sim$95\% out-of-sample accuracy; ablation tests indicate this information is embedded in long-timescale sSFH morphology rather than short-term variability. These results provide avenues for theoretical and observational follow-up connecting star formation histories to subgrid physical models.

**Comment:** Simulated star formation histories, theoretical SFH analysis

**Relevance:** 7

## 9. [From Giant Envelopes to Rapidly Contracting Cores in Rotating Population III Stars from $500$ to $1000 \, M_\odot$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17218) <a id="link9"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17218

**Authors:** Devesh Nandal, John Chisholm, Daniel J. Whalen, Andreas A. C. Sander, Volker Bromm, Peixin Zhu, Lisa J. Kewley

**Abstract:** Population III (Pop III) stars link the chemical enrichment of the early Universe to the formation of the first massive black holes, yet their evolution remains poorly understood at extreme masses. Using GENEC, we evolve rotating zero metallicity models from $500$ to $1000\,M_\odot$ at $v/v_{\rm crit}=0.1$ through core Si burning, and a $1000\,M_\odot$, $v/v_{\rm crit}=0.4$ model evolved through He exhaustion. In the fiducial $1000\,M_\odot$ model, convection and rotational transport carry freshly synthesized C and O into H burning layers, producing $62.1\,M_\odot$ of primary N by He exhaustion. Surface mass loss removes $20.2\,M_\odot$, including $1.8\times10^{-3}\,M_\odot$ of N, with $\log(\mathrm{N}/\mathrm{O})=0.68$, $\log(\mathrm{N}/\mathrm{C})=1.48$, and $\log(\mathrm{C}/\mathrm{O})=-0.80$. By the final hydrostatic model, the inner $100\,M_\odot$ is confined within $0.088\,R_\odot$ while the photosphere remains several thousand solar radii away. As C, Ne, O, and Si burning accelerate, chemical mixing freezes out and advanced products remain buried beneath the envelope. At core Si exhaustion, the central angular velocity reaches $4\times10^{-3}\,{\rm s^{-1}}$ while the surface rotates at $5.25\times10^{-8}\,{\rm s^{-1}}$, a contrast of $7.6\times10^4$. Contraction drives this spin up, while inner specific angular momentum changes by less than $1\%$. The maximum $j_{\rm eq}/j_{\rm ISCO}$ is $0.313$, limiting disk formation in our inside out estimate. These models show that Pop~III stars can produce strong primary N signatures and rapidly rotating black hole progenitors while their envelopes determine which products enrich the early Universe.

**Comment:** New stellar evolutionary tracks for massive Pop III stars

## 10. [Any colour you like: The diverse chromaticities of white dwarfs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18966) <a id="link10"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18966

**Authors:** Mark A. Hollands, Boris T. Gaensicke, Pier-Emmanuel Tremblay, Detlev Koester

**Abstract:** Main sequence stars have perceived colours ranging from orange to white to blue with increasing effective temperature. This is because their atmospheric opacities cause only minor deviations from blackbody colours when integrated over the broadband sensitivity curves of the human visual system. White dwarfs can have much denser atmospheres, reaching ~ 1 g/cm3 for the coolest known objects. If metals such as carbon, calcium, or sodium are present in these ultra-dense atmospheres, extreme broadening of atomic and molecular opacities can produce optical spectra that diverge significantly from blackbodies. We investigated how the combination of temperature and chemical composition can affect the perceived colours (chromaticity) of white dwarfs. While we found that most white dwarfs are similar in colour to blackbodies, we also demonstrate examples of known stars that would appear yellow, green, cyan, pink, or purple if viewed close enough for their stellar discs to be resolved. We examined the chromaticity distribution for the volume-limited 20 pc local sample of white dwarfs finding a median correlated colour temperature of ~= 6800 K, establishing that, on average, white dwarfs are indeed white in colour. Finally we show how white dwarf spectral models can be used to generate colour-accurate images of their stellar discs as they would appear from up close.

**Comment:** New white dwarf atmosphere models and colors

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 11. [Galactic Outflows and Star-Formation Quenching: A JWST Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19182) <a id="link11"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19182

**Authors:** Rebecca L. Davies, Sirio Belli

**Abstract:** Outflows driven by active galactic nuclei (AGN) are widely considered a key mechanism for regulating and quenching star formation in massive galaxies. However, direct observational evidence linking outflows to quenching has remained elusive for decades. This article reviews recent findings for massive galaxies at cosmic noon ($z\sim 2$), based on sensitive James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) observations tracing both star formation histories (through Balmer absorption lines) and neutral outflows (through blueshifted NaID absorption). Neutral outflows are found in $\sim$ 25 - 50% of massive ($\log(M_*/M_\odot)>$ 10) galaxies, independent of star formation rate. Outflows from star-forming galaxies are consistent with being powered by star-formation and/or AGN activity, whereas outflows from quiescent systems are generally attributed to current or previous AGN activity. There is growing evidence that recent rapid quenching of star-formation is associated with powerful AGN-driven outflows with mass loading factors $η\gtrsim$ 1 - 10. The neutral gas dominates the outflow mass budget but in most cases its velocity is below the halo escape velocity, indicating that this gas may form part of a galactic fountain. Detections of neutral gas inflows and residual cold gas reservoirs in quiescent galaxies suggest that other feedback mechanisms such as AGN heating are required to keep galaxies quenched over long timescales. We highlight the potential of multiphase outflow measurements to constrain feedback models, describe the main systematic uncertainties in mass outflow rate measurements, and discuss how the $z\sim 2$ results inform our understanding of the formation of the earliest massive quiescent galaxies.

**Comment:** Star-formation and AGN outflows, quenching, mass-loading factors

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 12. [Mergers may not be the main channel for galaxy stellar mass growth at redshift $z\gtrsim5$: Predictions from cosmological hierarchical models and simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17575) <a id="link12"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17575

**Authors:** Hao Fu, Mohammadreza Ayromlou, Francesco Shankar, Lars Hernquist, Andrea Lapi, Daniel Roberts, Feng Yuan, Maulik Bhatt, Evgenii Chaikin, Simona Gallerani, Luca Graziani, Antonio La Marca, George Mountrichas, Yingjie Peng, Haocheng Wang, Kai Wang

**Abstract:** Mergers are a crucial channel of galaxy growth, but their role relative to star formation remains uncertain at high redshift. Here we investigate merger rates and merger-driven stellar mass assembly for galaxies with stellar mass $M_\star \gtrsim 10^8 \, {\rm M_{\odot}}$ at $z\simeq 5-11$, by combining complementary theoretical approaches within $Λ$CDM. These include the DECODE semi-empirical model, the TNG100 and EAGLE hydrodynamical simulations, and the \textsc{L-Galaxies} semi-analytical model, together with the Millennium and TNG100-Dark simulations used to examine dark matter subhalo statistics and merger timescales. This compares models with different treatments of baryonic physics and galaxy-halo connections. We find that dark matter-only simulations predict merger timescales $\sim 30\%$ longer than those inferred from cosmological simulations including baryons. Galaxy major merger ($M_{\rm \star,sat} / M_{\rm \star,cen} > 0.25$) rates are high at cosmic dawn and increase towards earlier epochs, reaching $\sim 1/{\rm Gyr}$ at $z\sim 5$ and peaking at $\sim 3/{\rm Gyr}$ at $z\sim 8$. Despite these high rates, mergers contribute only $\lesssim 10\%$ of the total cosmic stellar mass density evolution. The ex-situ stellar mass fraction ranges from a few percent to $\sim 20\%$ for all galaxies at $z\gtrsim 5$, across different models and simulations. Their broad agreement implies that stellar mass assembly at cosmic dawn is dominated by in-situ star formation rather than mergers. These findings suggest that the high pair fractions identified by JWST may not directly trace efficient ex-situ stellar mass growth, highlighting the need for physically motivated merger timescales to interpret galaxy assembly in the early Universe.

**Abstract:** Precise and accurate measurements of host stars are crucial in the search for Earth-like exoplanets, and M-dwarfs are favourable targets in this quest. We measure a median precision for stellar companions of 4.2% in radius and 8.6% in mass within a sub-sample of 50 high-quality binaries, all with orbital periods $P_{\rm bin} > 5\,\rm days$. We compare our data to the Baraffe and MIST isochrones, and find our sample is statistically consistent with those models, producing reduced chi-squared values, $χ^2_ν\sim 1$. Our measured radius inflation is $0.3\pm 1.6$% indicating no statistically significant trend. We likewise detect no significant trend with metallicity, despite having the precision to identify such a relationship. As such, our observations do not appear to follow the general metallicity trend predicted in MIST. A $χ_ν^2 \sim 1$ indicates the scatter in our results is commensurate with our uncertainties alone. These results constrain the scope for unmodelled effects on the mass-radius relation that would produce radius deviations greater than 4.2%. **Authors:** Minjae Kim, Junhan Kim

**Abstract:** We study the intrinsic 0.5--2~keV emission of the hot circumgalactic medium (CGM) in the IllustrisTNG simulation TNG100-2 at redshifts 0, 0.1, and 0.2. X-ray luminosity increases with halo mass, although the class-dependent fits cover different mass intervals. Among galaxies with positive luminosity and star formation rate (SFR), the high-activity sample retains a positive association after a linear adjustment for halo mass. The residual association is weaker in the quiescent subset with positive luminosity and SFR. Within $10^{11.75}<M_{500}/M_\odot<10^{12.25}$, the high-activity sample has more centrally concentrated emission and a median luminosity about five times that of the quiescent sample. The two populations have similar median hot-gas masses despite their different X-ray luminosities and radial profiles. These differences are consistent with variations in the spatial distribution and thermodynamic state of the emitting gas, although the samples are not matched in halo mass. ## 15. [GroArnold: A Framework for Constructing Action-based Models for Triaxial Galaxies and Dark Matter Halos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18978) <a id="link15"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18978

**Authors:** Jian-Yu Gu, Xiao-Bo Dong, Shi-Yan Zhong, Run-Yu Meng

**Abstract:** Action-based distribution functions $f(\mathbf{J})$ provide a powerful description of stellar systems. Consequently, the construction of physically accurate and dynamically stable $f(\mathbf{J})$ for galactic components has emerged as a key challenge in the field. In this study, we present GroArnold, a computational framework designed to construct analytical $f(\mathbf{J})$ expressions for axisymmetric and triaxial galaxies and dark matter halos. The framework integrates modules for $N$-body initial condition generation, $N$-body simulation, angle--action variable estimation, and distribution-function fitting into a unified workflow. It supports a wide range of density profiles and kinematic properties, and is uniquely designed to generate stable $N$-body models for user-specified galactic systems, with the aim of obtaining an $f(\mathbf{J})$ that approximates a dynamically stable and physically consistent state. To this end, we extend the triaxial Stäckel Fudge method of the TACT code, implemented in our Nbody-TACT module, to enable action estimation directly from $N$-body gravitational potentials. GroArnold provides a robust and flexible tool for deriving numerically empirical, explicitly analytical distribution functions for user-specified galaxies or halos, thereby facilitating a wide range of practical $f(\mathbf{J})$-based modeling applications. In addition, it serves as a powerful tool for investigating the dynamics of realistic triaxial galaxies and halos beyond the scope of traditional spherical or axisymmetric models. We assessed the accuracy of the output $f(\mathbf{J})$ by comparing its predicted spatial and velocity distributions with those of the input $N$-body snapshot, and confirmed that it provides a reasonably accurate representation of the input. The code is modular, publicly available, and designed to support future extensions and adaptations.

**Authors:** Lurdes Ondaro-Mallea, Boryana Hadzhiyska, Raul E. Angulo

**Abstract:** The kinetic Sunyaev-Zel'dovich (kSZ) signal stacked around a galaxy sample traces the gas around halos and is a powerful probe of baryonic feedback. Its interpretation, however, depends on the satellite galaxies in the sample. Using the FLAMINGO hydrodynamical simulations and DESI-like samples of luminous red galaxies (LRGs), bright galaxies survey (BGS), and emission-line galaxies (ELGs), we show that satellites and feedback have similar effects: both suppress the small-scale signal. This degeneracy arises because stacking on satellites, which are offset from the centre of their host haloes, mimics the extended gas distribution produced by feedback. Although satellites make up only 20-30% of each sample, they account for 50-80% of the signal because they occupy more massive halos than centrals; thus their imprint on the stacked profiles can be as large as that of feedback. At the precision of current DESI$\times$ACT data, neglecting satellites in the modelling biases the inferred gas fractions low, by up to a factor of two, and overestimates the feedback strength, particularly for the LRG and BGS samples. Remaining agnostic about the satellite population avoids this bias but reduces the constraining power, so robust constraints would require knowing the satellite fraction to better than 10%, for instance from lensing and clustering data of the same galaxy samples. Finally, a further contribution from satellites will become statistically significant with upcoming surveys: satellites trace the anisotropic gas distribution around their host haloes, an effect that contributes 10% of the signal, and will likely require simulation-based models to capture.

**Novelty:** 6

## 17. [SQuIGGLE: Radio AGN Found Predominantly in Young, Gas-rich Post-starburst Galaxies at z ~ 0.7](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19146) <a id="link17"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19146

**Authors:** Vincenzo R. D'Onofrio, Justin S. Spilker, Krista L. Smith, Rachel Bezanson, Robert Feldmann, Alex J. Geiger, Andy D. Goulding, Jenny E. Greene, Mariska Kriek, Anika Kumar, Yuanze Luo, Desika Narayanan, David J. Setton, Katherine A. Suess, Margaret E. Verrico, Pengpei Zhu

**Abstract:** The processes that rapidly quench massive galaxies and maintain their quiescence remain poorly understood. Substantial molecular gas reservoirs can survive this shutdown in post-starburst galaxies, raising the question of what prevents the remaining gas from efficiently forming stars. We present deep, subarcsecond VLA 6 GHz observations of 52 massive SQuIGGLE post-starburst galaxies at $z\sim0.7$ to test the prevalence and mechanical impact of radio activity during the gas-rich post-burst phase. We detect 6 GHz continuum emission in 18/52 galaxies ($\approx$35\%), spanning $\log(L_{1.4\,{\rm GHz}}/{\rm W\,Hz^{-1}})\simeq23.0$--$25.0$ and generally exceeding the contributions expected from ongoing star formation and the fading starburst, favoring an AGN origin. Ancillary data at other wavelengths provide little evidence for a population of luminous, radiatively efficient AGN, suggesting that these sources are instead dominated by comparatively weak, mechanically powered radio AGN. Radio activity is strongly concentrated within the first $\sim$200 Myr post-burst and is predominantly associated with galaxies retaining substantial gas reservoirs. Above a common radio luminosity threshold, the radio detection fraction is enhanced by a factor of $\approx$2.5 relative to the radio AGN candidate fraction in mass- and redshift-matched quiescent LEGA-C galaxies, primarily due to lower power sources. For efficient jet--ISM coupling, the inferred mechanical power could supply the approximate energy required to unbind the molecular gas on timescales comparable to its observed decline. Together, these results identify a population of radiatively inefficient AGN hosted predominantly by young, gas-rich post-starburst galaxies at $z\sim0.7$ and suggest that they may have a role in maintaining inefficient star formation.

## 18. [Massive double-exponential galaxies with extended LSB emission: An IllustrisTNG exploration II -are they unique?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18576) <a id="link18"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18576

**Authors:** Suchira Sarkar, Kanak Saha, Anshuman Borgohain

**Abstract:** The massive disk galaxies (stellar mass >= 10^11 M_solar ) in the local universe (z < 0.1) encode challenging aspects of galaxy formation physics. We have studied one class of such galaxies - the giant low surface brightness galaxies (gLSBs) that show a hybrid morphology - a central Sersic, an inner high surface brightness, exponential stellar disk surrounded by an outer, extended low surface brightness disk, using large volume IllustrisTNG-100 simulation. This is motivated by the recent observation of several such systems and an earlier work by Sarkar & Saha (2025) from IllustrisTNG-50. We find that despite using a ~ 10 times larger volume simulation, only ~11% of massive disk galaxies show such morphology, consistent with findings from TNG50. This shows robustly that such galaxies are not common in the local universe. We compared them with the typical disk galaxies from observations, and single-exponential gLSBs from TNG100. We show that while the inner disk components lie well within the central surface brightness vs. radial scale length distribution of the typical disks, the outer LSB disks lie significantly at the outskirt, occupying a unique space. Their median bulge-to-total ratio is ~ 0.2 making them disk dominated, similar to the typical disks and the single-exponential gLSBs. We find their median dark matter mass fraction to be high (~ 0.9), typical of gLSBs. The outer LSB disks reach fainter central surface brightness and larger scale length values than the single-exponential gLSBs. Our exploration will be useful in understanding their formation mechanism across cosmic time.

## 19. [The Contribution of Mergers and Accretion to Dark Matter Halo Assembly: An Excursion Set Theory Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19312) <a id="link19"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19312

**Authors:** Hamed Kameli

**Abstract:** We study the relative contributions of mergers and accretion to the assembly of dark matter haloes within the framework of Excursion Set Theory (EST). We extend a variance-discretized merger-tree algorithm, which recursively reconstructs all branches of a complete merger tree and introduces a merger-ratio criterion for distinguishing mergers from accretion. The method is validated by comparing the resulting progenitor distributions with the analytical conditional first up-crossing distribution, showing excellent agreement over a wide range of halo masses and redshifts. We find that the accretion contribution depends strongly on the adopted merger-ratio threshold. For large merger ratios ($MR=0.1$--$0.25$), accretion accounts for up to $\sim70\%$ of the mass growth of massive haloes, whereas including all resolved mergers ($MR=10^{-6}$) reduces the accretion contribution to $\sim40\%$. These values are in good agreement with previous N-body simulation results. The accreted mass exhibits a nearly self-similar distribution in the mass-redshift plane, with the dominant contribution arising from progenitors in the range $10^{-3}M_0 \lesssim M \lesssim 10^{-1}M_0$ and predominantly at $0.5 \lesssim z \lesssim 2$. Our results demonstrate that EST-based merger trees provide an accurate and computationally efficient framework for investigating halo assembly and quantifying the relative roles of mergers and accretion in hierarchical structure formation.

## 20. [Substructure-enhanced Collisional Relaxation in Stellar Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18985) <a id="link20"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18985

**Authors:** Jian-Yu Gu, Xiao-Bo Dong, Run-Yu Meng

**Abstract:** Two-body collisional relaxation in stellar systems is conventionally estimated using the classical Chandrasekhar theory, in which a subject star is assumed to be embedded in a homogeneous medium of field stars and their velocity distribution taken to be isotropic and Gaussian. Real stellar systems, however, can exhibit pronounced spatial and kinematic substructures. We investigate how such substructures -- particularly the non-classical spatial inhomogeneity -- modify the two-body diffusion coefficient. We retain hyperbolic two-body encounters but generalize the encounter calculation by integrating explicitly over the spatial distribution $n(\mathbf{r})$, while evaluating the velocity contribution from the full distribution $f_v(\mathbf{v})$. This yields a separable tensor expression for the diffusion coefficient. We find that spatial substructure produces the dominant enhancement. The enhancement of the diffusion coefficient increases systematically as the effective fractal dimension $D_{\rm frac}$ decreases, $\log ζ_r(D_\text{frac}) \sim \left(1-D_\text{frac}/3\right)^q$ with $q \approx 4.28$. For the observationally motivated case with $D_{\rm frac}\approx 1.5$, the enhancement is moderate, whereas substantially lower fractal dimensions produce much larger enhancements. In contrast, the anisotropic, power-law-tailed, and fractal-like velocity distributions modify the diffusion coefficient only by factors of order unity. Our representative substructured model yields enhancement ratios $ζ_r\simeq3.45$ and $ζ_v\simeq1.33$ when the measured particle-number trends are extrapolated to $N=10^{11}$, corresponding to an overall enhancement of $ζ\simeq4.6$. Therefore, within the two-body encounter framework and the commonly used separable distribution functions, the enhanced collisional relaxation still has little impact on the mean-field nature of typical galactic systems.

**Novelty:** 5

## 21. [Unveiling the Hot Interstellar Medium in Nearby Galaxies with eROSITA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16936) <a id="link21"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16936

**Authors:** Roman Laktionov, Manami Sasaki, Elias Kyritsis, Andreas Zezas, Steven Hämmerich, Fiona Knies, Jörn Wilms, Frank Haberl, Martin G. H. Krause, Ashley Coombs, Philipp Weber, Aafia Zainab

**Abstract:** Diffuse X-ray emission in galaxies originates from stellar-feedback-heated gas and unresolved compact sources. We investigate its thermal component and the scaling of X-ray luminosity with star formation rate (SFR), including galaxies with very low SFRs. We analyzed eROSITA/SRG data from the four all-sky surveys (eRASS:4) for a blind, volume-limited sample of 140 galaxies within 25 Mpc from the Updated Nearby Galaxy Catalog (Karachentsev et al. 2013), cross-matched with HECATEv2 (Kyritsis et al. 2026b). Early-type galaxies were analyzed separately, while star-forming galaxies were divided into low-, medium-, and high-specific-SFR (sSFR) subsamples. We modeled the integrated X-ray spectra with thermal plasma and power-law components, stacked spectra for each subsample, and derived total and thermal X-ray luminosities. The stacked spectra show a stronger soft thermal component at high sSFR, whereas low-sSFR and early-type galaxies are increasingly dominated by the harder non-thermal continuum. For star-forming galaxies, the total 0.5-8 keV luminosity follows $\log L_X \sim 39.5 + 0.9\log\mathrm{SFR}$, with an intrinsic scatter of about 0.9 dex. The 0.5-2 keV thermal luminosity follows $\log L_X \sim 38.7 + 1.1\log\mathrm{SFR}$, with an intrinsic scatter of about 0.7 dex. The thermal luminosity remains well correlated with SFR down to $\sim 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{M}_\odot\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1}$ and is consistent with previous relations for star-forming galaxies. By contrast, the flatter and more scattered total X-ray luminosity-SFR relation suggests that unresolved X-ray binaries likely dominate at low SFR, while thermal X-ray emission remains a robust tracer of stellar feedback.

## 22. [TNGC-SIDM I: Evolution of massive cluster mergers under the self-interacting dark matter paradigm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18957) <a id="link22"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18957

**Authors:** Wonki Lee, Dylan Nelson, Myungkook J. Jee, Giulia Despali, Mark Vogelsberger, Annalisa Pillepich, Cian Roche, Kyle Finner, Hyungjin Joo, Daisuke Nagai, John ZuHone

**Abstract:** Merging galaxy clusters provide an ideal laboratory for testing the self-interacting nature of dark matter. In this paper, we present a pilot study of magnetohydrodynamical cosmological zoom-in simulations of two cluster mergers selected from TNG-Cluster, with $M_{\rm 200c} \sim 9\times10^{14}~M_\odot$ and $M_{\rm 200c}\sim 2\times10^{14}~M_\odot$. The simulations are performed with both standard collisionless cold dark matter (CDM) and self-interacting dark matter (SIDM), assuming an isotropic, velocity-independent cross-section of $σ_{\rm T}/m_χ= 1~\rm cm^2~g^{-1}$, with an additional $0.5~\rm cm^2~g^{-1}$ run for the low-mass system, and the mass resolution and galaxy formation model are unchanged from TNG-Cluster. We show how dark matter self-interactions produce an isothermal core in the dark matter halo, generate unique substructures in the intracluster light, and introduce variation in the multi-wavelength observables of the intracluster plasma. Using these simulations, we test the validity of observational probes for dark matter self-interactions in cluster mergers. We find that the shock-to-shock separation shows similar evolution in the self-interaction models tested here, providing a reference for distinguishing the $\sim100-200~\rm kpc$-scale difference in halo separation induced by self-interactions. Moreover, the dark matter halo centroid of the main cluster in the massive cluster merger is offset from the brightest cluster galaxy by as much as $\sim20~\rm kpc$, larger than the offset in the sub-cluster. This offset persists beyond 4 Gyr after the first pericenter passage. Our results indicate that sample selection plays a key role in testing the possible self-interacting nature of dark matter using cluster mergers, and that double radio relic cluster mergers and massive clusters with evidence of an ancient major merger offer a promising testbed for this purpose.

## 23. [Star spots mimic the proposed abundance signatures of planet formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18509) <a id="link23"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18509

**Authors:** Andrew R. Casey, Megan Bedell, Adam Wheeler, Julianne Dalcanton, Lily Zhao

**Abstract:** Trends with stellar elemental abundances and condensation temperature have been proposed as a possible tracer of rocky planet formation. Here we present an alternative explanation, where we show that stellar activity (star spots) can also cause apparent abundance changes in refractory elements preferentially over volatile elements. This effect arises naturally from atomic physics: refractory (rocky; high-$T_c$) elements preferentially have low first-ionization potentials, with strong transitions that are most sensitive to the cooler, magnetically-perturbed spot conditions, making any spot-induced change in line strength correlated with $T_c$. We re-examine literature on Sun-like stars in the Solar neighborhood and find that the $\log{R'_{HK}}$ stellar activity indicator is correlated with the so-called $T_c$ slope: the gradient of chemical abundances with respect to condensation temperature. Using 1D LTE Korg.jl simulations of the Sun with spots of varying size and contrast, we show that a wide range of spot configurations can manifest as abundance patterns that mimic a signal of abundance trends with condensation temperature. Finally, we conduct a line-by-line differential analysis of an ambient Solar spectrum relative to a Solar sunspot umbra to represent an extreme case, where we find that elements with high condensation temperature can show a factor of three (0.5 dex) increase. We discuss implications that stellar activity poses for precision spectroscopy, and provide some direction on how this effect might be mitigated.

## 25. [Non-equilibrium chemistry and radiation hydrodynamics in metal-poor and metal-rich interstellar gas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17577) <a id="link25"></a>

**Authors:** Shyam H. Menon, Piyush Sharda, Blakesley Burkhart, Tommaso Grassi

**Abstract:** We present a flexible implementation of non-equilibrium chemical networks with the $\texttt{KROME}$ framework, coupled to a nonlocal variable Eddington tensor-closed radiation transport scheme ($\texttt{VETTAM}$). Our fiducial network is designed to explicitly capture the formation pathways of important molecular species such as CO, OH, and $\rm H_2O$, intermediaries like $\rm H_3^+$, and the destruction of these species through photon and cosmic-ray dissociation. We validate our scheme through benchmark tests, and demonstrate its success in 1D and 3D problems covering a broad range of metallicities ($0 \leq Z \lesssim \rm Z_{\odot}$) and densities ($n \lesssim 10^{17} \, \rm cm^{-3}$). We confirm earlier works that suggest that OH and $\rm H_2O$ are the key molecular species in dense gas ($n \gtrsim 10^{5} \, \rm cm^{-3}$) for $Z\lesssim 0.01 \rm Z_{\odot}$, dominate the cooling at these densities, and demonstrate that our scheme can reproduce their abundances with similar accuracy as dedicated static photo-dissociation region (PDR) codes. We also find that the dominant fine-structure coolant in dense ($n \gtrsim 10^3 \, \, \rm cm^{-3}$) PDRs transitions from CI to CII to OI at increasingly lower $Z$. Finally, we find that in turbulent media, rapid mixing at PDR interfaces renders the chemical species out of equilibrium, and show that this could potentially alleviate the long-standing problem of (static) PDR models producing excess CI in the diffuse ISM over UV absorption-line measurements. We conclude by presenting and comparing with alternative implementations of reduced but faster chemical networks in our scheme, discussing the problem-dependent performance-accuracy tradeoffs and future prospects.

## 26. [A Nonlinear General Implicit Runge-Kutta Integrator for Multifluid Charged Dust and Gas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18979) <a id="link26"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18979

**Authors:** Mengxuan Chen, Hui Li, Chong-Chong He, Benjamin D. Wibking, Mark R. Krumholz, Xue-Ning Bai

**Abstract:** Charged dust in magnetized gas is subject to aerodynamic drag and the Lorentz force, both of which can be difficult to model numerically in a fluid simulation where their characteristic timescales are shorter than the timestep set by the Courant condition. We present a two-stage general implicit Runge-Kutta (GIRK) source integrator for multifluid charged dust coupled to an ideal magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) gas, implemented in the open source GPU-accelerated quokka code. Drag and Lorentz forces are advanced together in one implicit source update, avoiding errors in the terminal drift that can arise when the two forces are advanced in separate substeps. The timestep is compared with a drag-Lorentz timescale to select between resolved and stiff branches. The resolved branch uses Gauss-Legendre coefficients, while the stiff branch uses a coefficient set designed to recover the terminal drift set by the drag-Lorentz balance. We also allow the dust stopping time and charge-to-mass ratio to depend on the evolving state, evaluating them separately at the two implicit stage states and solving the resulting nonlinear stage equations by Picard iteration. The source update conserves total gas-dust momentum, and its computational cost scales linearly with the number of dust species. Tests reproduce the expected drag damping and terminal drift, confirm the predicted gyromotion amplitude and phase errors, demonstrate accurate treatment of state-dependent source coefficients, and recover the expected wave, shock, and instability dynamics in dusty MHD flows.

## 27. [Wave Cold Dark Matter Can Reproduce the Radial Acceleration Relation in X-ray Elliptical Galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18969) <a id="link27"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18969

**Authors:** Runyu Meng, Xiaobo Dong

**Abstract:** Wave cold dark matter (wave CDM) is a well-motivated variant within the collisionless CDM paradigm, in which the de~Broglie wavelengths of ultralight bosons become relevant on galactic scales. While many of the previous tests of wave CDM have focused on small-scale tensions involving solitonic cores and certain soliton--halo relations in galaxies, the radial acceleration relation (RAR) provides a complementary test: for a given baryonic mass distribution, it probes whether the DM halo can reproduce the observed total gravitational field. In this work we test wave CDM against the RAR using eight X-ray elliptical galaxies from the compilation of Lelli et al. We perform a suite of 3D Schrödinger--Poisson simulations and evolve the wave-CDM halos to virialized quasi-stationary states. For each galaxy and each adopted boson mass, we select the realization whose final snapshot most closely matches the empirical RAR. We find that the selected wave-CDM realizations for these elliptical galaxies can reproduce the RAR without including baryonic feedback or dark-matter self-interactions. At the boson mass $m_a=10^{-23}$eV, the combined fit to the eight galaxies gives $a_0=1.19\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, consistent with the canonical value inferred from disk galaxies. When the boson mass is varied over $3\times 10^{-24}$ eV $\le m_a \le 2\times 10^{-23}$ eV, the best-fit value of $a_0$ changes by only $\sim 11\%$, and this variation shows no systematic trend with $m_a$. This insensitivity to boson mass arises because, over the radial range relevant for the RAR, the virialized halos are supported mainly by their velocity field, whereas the boson-mass-dependent quantum-pressure term is dynamically subdominant. These results show that wave CDM can reproduce the RAR in elliptical galaxies, and provide a new galactic-scale test of the model beyond the previous focus on solitonic cores.

## 29. [Indirect Dark Matter Detection in the Multi-Messenger Era: Experiments, Systematics, Anomalies, and Prospects for Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17013) <a id="link29"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17013

**Authors:** Muad Ghaith, Khaled Obaideen

**Abstract:** Indirect searches for dark matter (DM) span gamma rays, charged cosmic rays, neutrinos, X-rays, radio emission, the cosmic microwave background (CMB), and the 21~cm signal, but reported constraints and anomalies vary in evidential strength. This critical review synthesizes peer-reviewed literature from January 2000 through October 2026 using a structured search strategy. We assess observations through a framework linking particle interactions and halo source terms to propagation or cosmological energy deposition, instrumental response, background modeling, and statistical inference. Fermi-LAT observations of dwarf spheroidal galaxies provide robust GeV-scale benchmarks for DM annihilation, whereas Galactic-center searches offer higher expected signals but depend more on foregrounds and halo profiles. Charged-cosmic-ray measurements are precise but limited by propagation and source uncertainties, while neutrino searches offer complementary sensitivity through solar capture, directional and flavor information, and high-mass annihilation or decay. Several widely discussed signals, including the 3.5~keV line, 511~keV emission, the Galactic-center GeV excess, charged-particle features, cluster radio excesses, and candidate gamma-ray lines remain inconclusive because of limited independent confirmation, uncertain target scaling, spectral degeneracies, or background-modeling limitations. Cosmological observations provide complementary constraints from CMB energy-injection limits and 21~cm measurements of the intergalactic medium. Artificial intelligence increasingly supports reconstruction and inference but requires rigorous calibration and domain-shift validation. The strongest case for DM discovery would require statistically consistent evidence across independent targets, instruments, or messengers, under common particle-physics parameters, and with well-characterized uncertainties.

**Novelty:** 5

## 30. [Intermittency in the Multiphase ISM --- I. Extreme Density, Velocity, and Magnetic Fluctuations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17342) <a id="link30"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17342

**Authors:** Yue Hu

**Abstract:** Intermittency is a fundamental property of astrophysical turbulence because it concentrates strong gradients, dissipation, and transport into rare, localized structures. In the multiphase interstellar medium (ISM), turbulent driving is especially important because the balance between compressive and solenoidal motions can regulate how extreme fluctuations are distributed among thermal phases. Yet conventional increment statistics quantify intermittency without identifying the structures that host it. We analyze three matched ideal-MHD simulations of a thermally multiphase neutral medium that differ only in the compressive fraction of the driving. Density, velocity, and magnetic-field increments are non-Gaussian in every thermal phase. Increasing compressive driving redistributes density and magnetic fluctuations preferentially into the cold gas rather than uniformly enhancing them across phases. We identify the structures associated with extreme increments using local, scale-matched diagnostics of compression, thermal gradients, vorticity, strain, magnetic curl, and magnetic tension. Extreme magnetic increments preferentially occur where magnetic curl and magnetic tension are simultaneously enhanced, more strongly than extreme velocity increments are associated with their kinematic hosts. Extreme velocity increments show comparable enrichment in vorticity and strain, whereas extreme density increments preferentially trace converging flows and thermal gradients only in the unstable medium. These associations weaken beyond a phase-dependent coherence scale. Our results show that turbulent driving controls how intermittency is partitioned among thermal phases, while distinct physical structures host extreme density, velocity, and magnetic fluctuations.

## 31. [The EBLM project - XX. Absolute dynamical masses of five low-mass eclipsing binaries, and an evaluation of mass estimation methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18339) <a id="link31"></a>

**Abstract:** Eclipsing binaries consisting of a solar-type primary and an M dwarf secondary are key laboratories to study objects at the bottom of the main sequence. Eclipsing binaries are essential to measure these parameters. However for stellar masses $<$ 0.35 M$_\odot$ , few double-lined eclipsing systems are known. Thus, stellar evolution models and empirical relations have had to rely on single-lined eclipsing binaries, for instance those from the EBLM project (Eclipsing Binary - Low Mass). Using high resolution spectroscopy to extract the weak signal of the secondary (flux ratio $<$ 1 per cent in the optical), we transform five more systems from single to double-lined, deriving absolute masses for both stars. We then compare methods typically employed to estimate stellar masses for field stars or single-lined binaries with the absolute dynamical masses we obtain, to assess their accuracy. Our results for SB1 and SB2 analyses are mostly consistent within 1-$σ$, though we identify a small systematic overestimation of SB1 masses derived from a combination of radial velocities and eclipse photometry. We show that the bias originates from primary radius overestimation during isochronal analysis, wherein the secondary light reduces photometric magnitudes and impacts the recovered radii by modifying the spectral energy distribution. With proper calibration, we can ensure the accuracy of using the full EBLM SB1 sample to constrain mass-radius-luminosity relationships for the smallest, yet most abundant, stars in the Galaxy

**Authors:** Robert Kincaid, Mark Sargent, Pascale Jablonka, Bruno Slaus, Viral Parekh, Shishir Sankhayan, Sameer Salunke, Jean-Paul Kneib

**Abstract:** The MeerKAT Observations of the Saraswati Supercluster (MOSS) is an ongoing project attempting to study the radio and optical properties of the core region of the Saraswati supercluster (z = 0.28) and will eventually entail a full survey of the entire supercluster region. Here we use radio catalogs from previous deep pilot MeerKAT L-band (1.28 GHz) observations (angular resolution of 8 arcsec) of the core region of the Saraswati supercluster. The region hosts two galaxy clusters representing distinct environments: The most massive cluster Abell 2631, and the second most massive cluster ZwCl2341, that is significantly more dynamically active. The deep radio catalogs of these clusters were combined with spectroscopic redshifts (mainly from DESI DR1 and SDSS DR16) and optical photometry catalogs of the cluster member galaxy source population. We use these redshifts together with the 1.4GHz MeerKAT flux densities to derive the dust-free star-formation rate (SFR) and evaluate various trends in the radio SFG and AGN galaxy populations. We find that the actively merging cluster ZwCL2341 has an increased star-forming galaxy population compared to A2631 with up to 4 times more star-forming galaxies. When looking at SFG-related radio processes we see s enhanced SF-related activity in ZwCL2341 with SF-related radio processes dominates entirely over AGN-related radio processes. This suggests that dynamically active environments associated with cluster assembly, such as mergers, have enhanced SF radio-activity and increased SFG (both radio and non-radio) galaxy population compared to lesser active systems.

## 34. [Nearly pristine gas in a galaxy 1 billion years after the Big Bang](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16509) <a id="link34"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16509

**Authors:** Sarah E. I. Bosman, Frederick B. Davies, Louise Welsh, Emanuele P. Farina, Prakash Gaikwad, Benedetta Spina, Barun Maity, Ryan Cooke, Klaudia Protušová, Valentina D'Odorico, Eduardo Bañados, James S. Bolton, Rebecca L. Davies, Xiaohui Fan, Martin G. Haehnelt, Joseph F. Hennawi, Tahlia J. Hill, Seyeon Hwang, Vid Iršič, Laura Keating, Girish Kulkarni, Samuel Lai, Emma V. Ryan-Weber, Lasse Seyberlich, Molly Wolfson

**Abstract:** Excitation of carbon and oxygen ions is the primary mechanism by which collapsing gas clouds dissipate heat to form new stars. At early cosmic times, in galaxies with chemically pristine gas, many stars are expected to have masses far larger than the mass of our sun due to the deficit in these elements. We searched for ultra-metal-poor galaxies using their spectroscopic absorption signatures in front of bright background quasars, employing the new GHOST high-resolution spectrograph. Here we report the discovery of the first galaxy containing a reservoir of neutral hydrogen gas with metallicity below the ultra-metal-poor threshold: with a measured carbon-to-hydrogen ratio 11,000 times lower than the Sun, it is the most pristine ever found by a factor of four. UMP-DLA-1 is the only known galaxy with measured carbon and oxygen abundances too low to enable efficient formation of stars with solar mass or below. The low metallicity alone therefore guarantees a top-heavy stellar population, providing a direct view of the chemical composition imprinted by massive low-metallicity stars that perished long before the present day: an elevated oxygen-to-carbon ratio. The abundance pattern of UMP-DLA-1 is likely a close representation of the atomic yields of first- or early second-generation stars.

## 35. [Differential Luminosity Response of Balmer-Line Profiles in Galactic B/A Luminous Stars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18810) <a id="link35"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18810

**Authors:** N. L. Vaidman, S. A. Khokhlov, Sh. T. Nurmakhametova, N. A. Litvinov, A. T. Agishev, D. A. Alimbetova

**Abstract:** We investigate the differential luminosity response of the optical Balmer series from H8 to H-alpha in Galactic B5-A5 luminous stars using homogeneous measurements of central depth, FWHM, and equivalent width at a resolving power of approximately 12,000. Direct line-to-line comparisons show a systematic change in the central-depth response toward the lower-order transitions. For the primary 72-star luminosity sample, the fitted luminosity coefficients show an overall positive trend with upper-level quantum number, with a point-estimate gradient of dq/dn = 0.0419. A joint Monte Carlo analysis with 10,000 realisations gives a median dq/dn = 0.0387 and a 95 percent interval from 0.0253 to 0.0532, with no non-positive gradients obtained. The higher-order H8-H-delta coefficients form a statistically unresolved plateau, followed by a well-supported increase in response magnitude from H-delta toward H-alpha. The result is robust to the adopted effective-temperature scale. The FWHM coefficients show no comparable Balmer-order progression, and the directly fitted profile-shape response is consistent with zero from H8 through H-beta. For H-alpha, the full logarithmic sample is not formally significant, whereas removal of the three remaining strongly filled-core profiles yields a significantly negative shape response. The observed behaviour is qualitatively compatible with the systematic variation of pressure broadening across the Balmer series, while the enhanced H-alpha response may additionally reflect non-LTE and wind-related effects.

## 36. [Double Detonations on a Moving Mesh with $\texttt{Cattails}$: Characterizing Early Reddening in SNe Ia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16899) <a id="link36"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16899

**Authors:** Dillon Hasenour, Paul Duffell, Abigail Polin

**Abstract:** Type Ia supernovae are useful as cosmological distance indicators and are significant sources of chemical enrichment in galaxies, but much about their origins remains uncertain. The double detonation explosion mechanism is one promising model for these transients. We present a suite of thermonuclear hydrodynamic double detonation models of sub-Chandrasekhar mass white dwarfs spanning thin to thick helium shells. Thin shell models produce light curves and spectra consistent with normal Type Ia supernovae, and thicker shell models have early time luminosity bumps from radioactive ashes in the shell and early reddening in color sometimes observed in peculiar Type I supernovae. The reddening is due to line blanketing from heavy elements produced in the shell detonation encompassing the central emission. We perform the computations using $\texttt{Cattails}$, a moving mesh nuclear hydrodynamics code introduced in this work. After carrying out subsequent radiative transfer calculations using the $\texttt{Sedona}$ code, we relate the time of early reddening to the mass of the initial helium shell. We also consider how the relation changes as a function of total mass as well as how the peak brightness depends on the total mass. Finally, we provide a series of fitting functions that can be used to estimate the mass of the shell consistent with an observation of reddening in the early color evolution of a type Ia supernova.

## 37. [The Atacama Cosmology Telescope: DR6 Constraints on Isotropic Time-Dependent Cosmic Birefringence from Axion-like Particles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19049) <a id="link37"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19049

**Authors:** Zachary B. Huber, Huajian Yang, Steve K. Choi, Erminia Calabrese, Vera Gluscevic, Sigurd Naess, Michael D. Niemack, Lyman A. Page, Alexander van Engelen, Edward J. Wollack

**Abstract:** Axion-like particles (ALPs) with sufficiently low masses are dark matter candidates because they could account for much or all of the observed dark matter energy density and help explain observations of galactic substructure. Such pseudoscalar fields could couple to photons, causing a time-dependent rotation of the polarization of the light with an oscillation frequency equal to the ALP mass. We use measurements of the polarization of the cosmic microwave background (CMB) by the Atacama Cosmology Telescope (ACT) between 2017 and 2022 to search for this effect using an EB power spectrum estimator. By probing oscillation periods from days to years, the data constrain the coupling between ALPs and photons for ALP masses between $2.5\times 10^{-23}$ eV and $4.8\times 10^{-20}$ eV. We find no evidence for such a signal and set a 95% upper bound on the axion-photon coupling constant, $g_{φγ} < 1.1\times10^{-11}\left({m_φ}/{10^{-21}\,\mathrm{eV}}\right)\,\mathrm{GeV}^{-1} .$ This limit assumes that ALPs make up all of the dark matter and that the ALP field amplitude is stochastically distributed, which leads to more conservative bounds than the deterministic-field assumption used in some previous CMB polarization analyses. Among searches for ALP-photon coupling using CMB polarization, we set the strongest bounds to date under the stochastic-field assumption. This work develops a methodology to analyze over 10,000 time-sliced "depth-1" polarization maps from ACT, a data product that will be standard for time-domain astrophysics with the Simons Observatory, enabling searches with a higher sensitivity and observing span.

## 38. [The Dance of the Little Red Dots: Spectroscopy of Two Blue Companions at z = 3.9 and 5.5 Reveals Young, Metal-Poor Starbursts Merging with Their LRDs within a Few Hundred Myr](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18968) <a id="link38"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18968

**Authors:** Fabio Pacucci, Pierluigi Rinaldi

**Abstract:** The Little Red Dots (LRDs) are compact, red sources, abundant at $4 \lesssim z \lesssim 9$ and likely powered by massive black holes. Photometric studies have found blue companions around them, but none has yet been confirmed spectroscopically. This Letter presents the only two LRD-companion pairs with spectroscopy of both members in the literature: "Waltz" at $z = 3.9$ and "Tango" at $z = 5.5$, with projected separations of $3.6$ and $2.5$ kpc. Both companions lie at the redshift of their LRD, within $132 \, \rm km \, s^{-1}$ and $57 \, \rm km \, s^{-1}$, and are physically identical, but $524$ Myr apart: starbursts $\lesssim 50$ Myr old, metal-poor ($0.2 \, \rm Z_\odot$), dust-free, dynamically cold, and as bright as the LRDs themselves in the rest-frame UV. The LRD in Waltz hosts a $\approx 3 \times 10^{6} \, \rm M_\odot$ black hole accreting at half the Eddington rate, a factor of a few above the birth mass of a direct collapse black hole, and is embedded in gas as metal-poor as its companion. Orbital modeling shows that Tango is bound in $81\%$ of the allowed orbits and that Waltz is bound if its host halo exceeds $5 \times 10^{10} \, \rm M_\odot$. Both blue companions then merge with their respective LRD within a few hundred Myr. We conclude that we are observing what heavy seeding requires: a young black hole beside the metal-poor starburst that the synchronized-pair scenario predicts, in the last steps of a cosmic dance that ends in a merger, and in a galaxy seeded by its black hole.

## 39. [DEMOS. II. What the full Lupus demographics truly tell us about protoplanetary disc evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17284) <a id="link39"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17284

**Authors:** Lorenzo A. Malanga, Giovanni P. Rosotti, Giuseppe Lodato, Alice Somigliana, Benoît Tabone, Rossella Anania, Carlo F. Manara, Leonardo Testi, Claudia Toci, Francesco Zagaria

**Abstract:** Assessing whether disc evolution is driven by viscosity or MHD winds is crucial to determine the conditions in which planets form. The rich statistical information encoded in nearby star-forming regions provides an excellent opportunity to probe disc evolution. In this paper, we apply the Bayesian inference tool DEMOS, designed to exploit the statistical properties of disc populations and infer initial conditions and model parameters, to the Lupus disc population. We consider the observed distributions of stellar mass $M_\star$, disc mass $M$ (from continuum flux), mass accretion rate $\dot{M}$ and CO disc size $R_{90%, CO}$. We constrain all parameters for both the viscous model, with internal photoevaporation, and the MHD winds model, with unprecedented precision. The inferred initial conditions suggest that positive correlations between initial disc properties and $M_\star$ must be present. Both models yield a typical $R_0$ of $3-5$ au for a disc around a $0.3\ \text{M}_\odot$ star, with positive stellar-mass dependence. We infer $α$ (or $α_\text{DW}$) of $3-4 \times 10^{-4}$ for discs around $0.3\ \text{M}_\odot$ stars, with an approximately quadratic dependence on $M_\star$. For the viscous-photoevaporative model, the typical wind mass-loss rate is $\dot{M}_\text{w} \sim 10^{-9}$ M$_\odot$ yr$^{-1}$, scaling with $M_\star$ with an exponent of $\sim 2.5$. The inferred mean age is 1.6 Myr for the MHD winds model and 0.9 Myr for the viscous-photoevaporative model. Despite a slight preference for the latter, Bayesian evidence shows that both models reproduce the data comparably well, and neither can be ruled out. However, their best-fit synthetic populations predict substantially different evolutionary pathways. Applying DEMOS to multiple populations, especially evolved ones like Upper Scorpius, will allow to place stronger constraints on disc evolution mechanisms.

## 40. [WALLABY pilot survey: accurate and automated linewidth measurements from global HI profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16057) <a id="link40"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16057

**Authors:** Shinna Kim, Tobias Westmeier, Lister Staveley-Smith, Danail Obreschkow, Barbara Catinella, Nathan Deg, Karen Lee-Waddell, Jonghwan Rhee, Kristine Spekkens, O. Ivy Wong, Matthew Colless, Hélène M. Courtois, Boyi Ding, Bumhyun Lee

**Abstract:** Current-generation 21-cm surveys of extragalactic neutral hydrogen (HI) with instruments such as FAST and ASKAP are providing detailed spectral and spatial information for over 10^5 galaxies. Future HI surveys with next-generation radio telescopes could provide sample sizes larger by orders of magnitude. There is therefore a need for accurate, automated, and reliable parameterisation of the HI emission from galaxies. We have developed an automated estimator for HI global profile widths to better enable studies of, for example, dynamical masses and Tully-Fisher distances. Our estimator is based on an implementation of the BusyFit profile-fitting algorithm, and converges for 95% of global profiles in the WALLABY pilot survey. Probability density functions are calculated for all profile parameters using Monte Carlo techniques, and example error parameterisations are given. We confirm that linewidth uncertainties depend primarily on the integrated signal-to-noise ratio, profile steepness, and the linewidth, consistent with previous studies. We compare several common linewidth definitions, and the width at 50% of the mean flux density (Wm50) is found to be the most robust measure of linewidth. We provide examples of the baryonic Tully-Fisher relation drawn from the WALLABY pilot survey.

## 41. [Chandra-JWST Probing the Effects of Moderate X-Ray Superflares on Protoplanetary Disks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19228) <a id="link41"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19228

**Authors:** Konstantin V. Getman, Kevin L. Luhman, Bayron Portilla-Revelo, Christian Rab, Valentin Brunn, Eric D. Feigelson, Arjan Bik, Jan Forbrich, Jenny Frediani, Thomas J. Haworth, Charles J. Law, Marco Padovani, Maria Claudia Ramirez-Tannus, Andrew J. Winter, Vladimir S. Airapetian, L. Ilsedore Cleeves, Sergio A. Dzib, Michael A. Kuhn, Abygail R. Waggoner, Rens Waters

**Abstract:** Powerful X-ray flares from young stars may rapidly alter the physical and chemical conditions of their planet-forming disks, but direct observational tests remain scarce. We present a Chandra-JWST/NIRSpec study of infrared spectral variability associated with pre-main-sequence (PMS) X-ray flares in the Orion Nebula. Combining six Chandra/ACIS-I observations with three NIRSpec-MOS visits, we analyze two-epoch spectra of 10 X-ray Flare stars and 12 matched non-flaring Controls among 84 observed disk-bearing PMS stars. We investigate three diagnostics: narrow 2.8-4.3 micron features, HI Br-alpha, and the CO fundamental band. Flare stars show tentative evidence for relatively fewer variable narrow features at longer wavelengths than Controls, while feature incidence decreases strongly with projected distance from the dominant ionizing O star Theta1 Ori C. Most classifiable features appear in absorption. These properties suggest that at least some features may arise in externally irradiated disk atmospheres and/or associated photoevaporative flows, with their apparent epoch-to-epoch variability potentially reflecting spatial sampling of such locally structured material rather than intrinsic temporal changes. Br-alpha behaves differently: flare stars show systematically stronger variability and uniquely exhibit absorption-core profiles, suggesting control by magnetospheric accretion and stellar magnetic processes rather than disk irradiation. The CO fundamental band shows no significant flare-associated variability, consistent with thermochemical models predicting weak responses to moderate X-ray superflares. This pilot study demonstrates the potential of coordinated Chandra-JWST observations to probe how young stellar magnetic activity affects planet-forming environments, motivating future monitoring to build larger superflare samples and extend them into the rare X-ray mega-flare regime.

## 42. [Emergence of high-mass stars in complex fiber networks (EMERGE) VII. Multi-Tracer Column Density Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19163) <a id="link42"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19163

**Authors:** Alvaro Hacar, Andrea Socci, Mario Tafalla, Francesca Bonanomi

**Abstract:** Obtaining accurate estimates of the gas column density $\mathrm{N(H_2)}$ is crucial to determine the physical conditions of the molecular gas in the Interstellar Medium. This paper introduces the "Multi-Tracer Column Density Synthesis" (MT-CDS) technique, a new empirical approach to derive high dynamic-range column density maps with interferometers such as ALMA. We have compared the integrated intensity maps of two density selective tracers, namely HNC (1-0) and $\mathrm{N_2H^+}$ (1-0), obtained with IRAM-30m with ancillary Herschel column density estimates in seven star-forming regions in Orion and derived individual temperature-corrected conversion factors for each of these tracers. We applied these conversion factors obtained at low-resolution into similar high sensitivity ALMA observations to derive independent column density maps describing both the low $N_\mathrm{HNC}\mathrm{(H_2)}$ and high $N_\mathrm{N_2H^+}\mathrm{(H_2)}$ column density components at high spatial resolution (4.5"). We also combined these independent maps into estimates for the total gas column density gas $N_\mathrm{tot}\mathrm{(H_2)}$ sampling more than two orders of magnitude in column density between $\sim 10^{21.5}-10^{23.5}$ cm$^{-2}$. A comparison with independent ArTeMiS column density maps at intermediate resolution (8") shows typical deviations of $\lesssim$0.2 dex from our MT-CDS gas column density estimates. We obtained individual column density maps in five of our targets at $\sim$2000 au resolution in which ALMA observations were available, that is a factor of $\sim$5 better than originally calibrated. These results demonstrate the potential of this MT-CDS technique to retrieve accurate, high dynamic range $N\mathrm{(H_2)}$ total gas column density estimates at interferometric resolution combining multiple molecular line tracers.

## 43. [Thermodynamically consistent equation of state tables for use in giant planet and brown dwarf interior modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18982) <a id="link43"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18982

**Authors:** Emily Sandford, Yamila Miguel

**Abstract:** Equation of state (EOS) tables used in giant planet modeling exhibit regions of strong thermodynamic inconsistency, or disobedience to the first law of thermodynamics. These generally result from smooth interpolation over physical discontinuities or between thermodynamic sub-domains governed by different physical models. The effect of thermodynamic inconsistency on structure models of planets evolved with these EOS has never been studied in detail, although it is understood to introduce spurious sources or sinks of energy or entropy in the models, leading to inaccuracies. Here, we present a method for improving the thermodynamic consistency of an existing EOS table, based on constructing a Helmholtz free energy $F(ρ,T)$ covering the EOS domain and deriving all the other necessary EOS quantities from it. This method produces EOS tables with roughly thousandfold better thermodynamic consistency across the EOS domain of interest, as quantified by three thermodynamic consistency metrics derived from the first law. We also present a controlled experiment comparing giant planet models evolved with MESA using the original EOS and the $F$-derived, thermodynamically consistent versions, and find that the models can differ by up to $5\%$ in radius at an age of 5 Gyr, and by up to $25\%$ in luminosity at earlier stages of evolution; thermodynamic consistency is thus an especially important consideration for characterising young directly imaged planets. We present the $F$-derived, thermodynamically consistent versions of the tables for download at \href{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21103561}{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21103561}, along with example MESA inlists to reproduce our evolution models.
