Lamagna, M. Lattanzi, C. Leloup, M. Lembo, F. Levrier, A. I. Lonappan, M. López-Caniego, D. Maino, V. Maranchery, S. Masi, S. Matarrese, S. Micheli, M. Migliaccio, M. Monelli, L. Montier, G. Morgante, M. Morshed, L. Mousset, M. Najafi, T. Namikawa, P. Natoli, I. Obata, A. Occhiuzzi, A. Paiella, D. Paoletti, S. Paradiso, G. Pascual-Cisneros, F. Piacentini, G. Piccirilli, M. Pinchera, G. Polenta, L. Porcelli, M. Reinecke, M. Remazeilles, A. Ritacco, J. A. Rubiño-Martín, M. Ruiz-Granda, Y. Sakurai, L. Salvati, J. Sanghavi, D. Scott, D. Shenoy, M. Shiraishi, G. Signorelli, S. Stellati, R. M. Sullivan, K. Tassis, L. Terenzi, M. Tomasi, H. Tran, L. Vacher, B. van Tent, P. Vielva, S. Vinzl, D. J. Watts, I. K. Wehus, G. Weymann-Despres, for the LiteBIRD Collaboration

**Abstract:** The LiteBIRD satellite mission aims to measure the tensor-to-scalar ratio $r$ with unprecedented sensitivity through observations of the cosmic microwave background (CMB) $B$-mode polarisation. Robust constraints on $r$ require accurate likelihood modelling across a wide range of angular scales. In this work, we validate harmonic-space likelihood methods against pixel-based benchmarks using 20\,000 end-to-end simulations that include CMB, instrumental noise, and Galactic foreground residuals processed through a realistic component-separation pipeline. We compare a debiased pixel-based likelihood, which serves as our benchmark on large scales ($\ell\leq32$), with an offset Hamimeche \& Lewis (oHL) likelihood applicable across the full multipole range. We systematically explore the space of analysis settings, identifying the number of simulations used for covariance estimation and the choice of beam window function as the most critical factors. Over the full multipole range considered ($\ell\leq128$), where the benchmark combines the pixel-based likelihood at $\ell\leq32$ with a harmonic one at smaller scales, the 68\% upper bound on $r$ from the oHL likelihood is $19$--$29\%$ larger than the benchmark one, depending on the beam window function. This demonstrates its viability as a computationally efficient baseline for LiteBIRD data analysis and cosmological inference from the polarisation $B$-mode field.

## 145. [Discovery of 51 pulsars in a reanalysis of the Green Bank North Celestial Cap Survey and a revised pulsar census](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17280) <a id="link145"></a>

**Authors:** R. Sengar, L. Vleeschower, D. L. Kaplan, G. Y. Agazie, M. Bailes, A. Cassity, H. T. Cromartie, M. E. Decesar, T. Dolch, W. Fiore, E. Fonseca, R. S. Lynch, E. Parent, S. M. Ransom, I. H. Stairs, J. van Leeuwen

**Abstract:** We present results from a reanalysis of the Green Bank North Celestial Cap (GBNCC) survey, conducted at 350 MHz with the Green Bank Telescope between 2009 and 2020, covering the sky with declinations $δ>-40^\circ$. We searched the survey data using an FFT-based periodicity search, with candidate selection extending to very low spectral signal-to-noise ratios and summing up to 32 harmonics. From the 60 promising candidates identified in this search, we confirmed 51 new pulsars using the Green bank and CHIME. We also used a direct folding search method (DFSM) to search for slow pulsars between 500 ms and 10 s. Of the 51 pulsars identified in the FFT search, 43 fall within this period range, all of which were found by the DFSM. The DFSM also found an additional pulsar that had been missed during the inspection of the FFT candidates. Most of the new discoveries are canonical pulsars, with spin periods ranging from 299 ms to 4.4 s and dispersion measures from 10 to 192 $\rm pc \, cm^{-3}$. We also found a binary millisecond pulsar with a spin period of 3.2 ms in a 4.5-d orbit. We redetected 855 pulsars in the data, 825 of which were found in the FFT search. Within the effective parameter space of our periodicity search, the FFT search recovered $\sim$98 % of the known pulsars within the survey sensitivity limits. After accounting for previously reported pulsars whose observations were not processed or whose parameters lay outside our search range, the updated GBNCC census now includes 964 pulsars.

## 146. [Neural networks applied to forecasting the astroclimatic and meteorological parameters in an operational context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19070) <a id="link146"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19070

**Authors:** C. Weinberger, E. Masciadri, M. De Sepibus, A. Otarola, S. Miesske, C. Veillet, S. Ragland

**Abstract:** Forecasting optical turbulence and atmospheric parameters is crucial for science operations of present and new generation ground-based telescopes (ELT) as well as for free-space optical communication (FSOC). These forecasts are also useful to optimize the AO use, particularly in the ELT framework too. Recent research has demonstrated that the AR technique currently provides the best performances in predicting atmospheric and astroclimatic parameters on a time scale of 1 hour (e.g., RMSE 0.1" for the seeing), however deep learning offers some intriguing features that include the high adaptability, the correlation that can be found between different parameters treated simultaneously and different architectures can explore the capability for possible improvements. For this reason, it is important to investigate on the possibility to improve forecast performances beyond the score achieved by AR or simply to apply deep learning in those context in which it is not possible to apply the AR. In this contribution we investigate a set of algorithms belonging to the family of the neural networks that in principle are more suitable than the Random Forest (RF) model for this kind of application. We address specific questions related to the forecast performances in operational configurations. Besides that, we also investigate the impacts on gaps on the trained data set due to the lack of measurements of the optical turbulence during the day time. We show evidences that with measurements covering the whole 24-hours forecast performances might improve. Our test has been done on the wind speed in this contribution. This work is done in the framework of the operational forecast system for LBT (ALTA Center) and for the VLT (FATE project) that are leaded by our team.

## 147. [Scalar-Assisted Growth of Supermassive Black Holes: Boson-Mass Windows and Observational Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18986) <a id="link147"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18986

**Authors:** Jorge Castelo Mourelle, Darío Núñez Zúñiga, Saeed Fakhry, Nicolas Sanchis-Gual

**Abstract:** We study a phenomenological cosmological model of black-hole growth in which a finite baryonic reservoir and a bosonic scalar cloud feed the black hole simultaneously. The baryonic component accretes through an Eddington-like channel, while the scalar cloud transfers mass through an effective quasibound-state decay rate. Evolving the system directly in redshift within a flat $Λ$CDM background, we scan over $μ$, $M_0$, $M_{c,0}$, and $M_{b,0}$ to assess whether scalar-assisted accretion can accelerate the formation of $10^9M_\odot$ supermassive black holes. We find that the scalar contribution is highly selective in parameter space. For the mass scales explored here, scalar transfer is most efficient for $μ\sim10^{-19}-10^{-16} \mathrm{eV}$, producing rapid cloud depletion and significantly earlier black-hole growth, while outside this interval the evolution remains mainly baryonic. More generally, this efficient window reflects a preferred range of the dimensionless coupling $α=μM_{\rm BH}$ and therefore shifts approximately as $μ\propto M_{\rm BH}^{-1}$. Comparison with observed quasar masses and luminosities instead favors lower boson masses, broadly around $μ\sim10^{-21}-10^{-20}\mathrm{eV}$. These results are not necessarily in tension, since the boson mass that maximizes early growth depends on the characteristic black-hole mass and evolutionary stage. This separation between dynamical efficiency and observational compatibility leaves open complementary roles for different bosonic mass scales in supermassive black hole growth and dark-matter phenomenology.

## 148. [Delving into the depths of NGC 3783 with XRISM VI. Evidence for a failed wind based on Fe xxv absorption line variability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18429) <a id="link148"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18429

**Authors:** Chen Li, Jelle S. Kaastra, Liyi Gu, Missagh Mehdipour, Keigo Fukumura, Matteo Guainazzi, Misaki Mizumoto, Ehud Behar, Shoji Ogawa, Keqin Zhao

**Abstract:** NGC 3783 exhibits a prominent Fe XXV absorption trough in the time-averaged XRISM/Resolve spectrum. To investigate its short-term variability, we present a time-resolved analysis of the Fe XXV absorption line during the continuous 10-day observation. We divided the resolved absorption profile into five fixed velocity intervals and measured their normalized equivalent widths in 19 time-resolved spectra. Three velocity intervals exhibit significant equivalent-width (EW) variability. Two velocity intervals respond differently to low- and high-flux states with less emission line contamination. The time-dependent photoionisation modelling is unable to reproduce the most significant observed EW variation ($3.5 \, σ$), possibly owing to uncertainties in the assumed initial equilibrium state, which prevent a precise density measurement. Instead, using photoionisation calculations and assuming the high-density limit, we derive approximate constraints on the ionisation parameters of the two relevant components. The low-velocity component (-320 to -680 km/s) shows a delayed inverse response, indicating a high ionisation parameter, while its relatively short lag timescale indicates a relatively high density and a distance upper limit of 0.05 pc. In contrast, the high-velocity component (-680 to -1040 km/s) manifests a positive response with a longer lag to the ionising continuum, implying a relatively low ionisation parameter. This component appears to be less dense, with an inferred distance upper limit of 0.25 pc. The low-velocity component is consistent with a failed wind, as its outflow velocity is below the local escape velocity, whereas the high-velocity component lies near the escape threshold. Together with the lower-ionisation failed wind detected in 2001, this supports a multiphase failed-wind scenario in the unobscured state of NGC~3783 over more than two decades.

## 149. [Particle Selection in Virtual Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17094) <a id="link149"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17094

**Authors:** Boaz Keren-Gil, James Gain, Lucia Marchetti, Patrick Marais, Alexander Sivitilli

**Abstract:** Point-cloud representations of 3D data are used in many fields, from astronomical surveys of galaxies to medical imaging and laser scans of buildings. A task common to all of these is interactive selection: picking out a meaningful subset of points for closer analysis. The data is inherently 3D, yet the traditional workflow uses a 2D mouse on a 2D projection and inherits the limitations of a flat screen. This paper compares Virtual Reality (VR) and desktop environments for 3D particle selection, asking whether VR's six-degree-of-freedom hand interaction yields measurable gains in accuracy, speed, usability, workload and flow. In a counterbalanced within-subjects experiment (N = 34), participants selected 90 target galaxies from a catalogue of 16,816 galaxies, using mouse-driven selection with keyboard camera control on the desktop and a hand-held spherical brush that adds or removes particles in VR. We measured task accuracy, completion time, and three standard questionnaires: the System Usability Scale (SUS), the NASA Task Load Index (NASA-TLX) and the Flow State Scale (FSS). Accuracy was comparable (91.6% VR vs. 93.1% desktop; p = 0.858), but every other measure favoured VR. Tasks were 23% faster (p = 0.001, d = 0.93), usability was higher (SUS 84.6 vs. 54.0; d = -1.78), workload was lower (NASA-TLX 20.4 vs. 34.7; d = 0.90), and flow was higher (FSS 83.2 vs. 70.2; d = -1.23). Because the underlying problem, making a 3D selection through a 2D interface, is not specific to astronomy, we expect the pattern to carry over to point-cloud selection in other fields.

## 150. [Radiation-Regulated Spherical Accretion with an Inner Accretion Disk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16097) <a id="link150"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16097

**Authors:** Cheng-Liang Jiao, Liying Zhu, Xiang-dong Shi, Er-gang Zhao

**Abstract:** We develop a theoretical model for radiation-regulated spherical accretion with an inner accretion disk. In our model, the outer accretion flow remains quasi-spherical and optically thick, while the luminosity generated by the inner disk is self-consistently coupled to the accretion rate and irradiates the inflowing gas. By solving the global transonic structure with radiative transfer treated in the diffusion approximation, we investigate how radiation regulates the flow structure and the accretion rate. We find that the luminosity is constrained by a generalized Eddington limit, leading to a saturated accretion rate typically lower than the classical Bondi prediction by orders of magnitude. At sufficiently low densities, the enthalpy flux of the spherical inflow contributes substantially to the emergent luminosity, in which case the inner disk luminosity is reduced and the accretion flow enters an unsaturated state. A criterion is derived to estimate whether the accretion flow is saturated. We further demonstrate the applicability of our model by considering fallback accretion in failed supernova systems. The predicted nearly constant emergent luminosity, constrained by the generalized Eddington limit, provides a possible explanation for the observed luminosity plateau in the failed supernova candidate M31-2014-DS1. The presence of an inner disk also provides sufficient radiation to avoid the under-luminous predictions of a purely spherical fallback model extending down to the central object. Our model provides a framework for studying radiation-regulated accretion flows in systems where low but finite angular momentum leads to the formation of an accretion disk within a larger-scale quasi-spherical inflow.

**Novelty:** 4

## 151. [Beyond the Kullback--Leibler divergence: the likelihood-ratio distribution as a detectability measure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17186) <a id="link151"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17186

**Authors:** Amirhossein Samandar, Mikel Martin Barandiaran, Linn Htat Lu, Andrew H. Jaffe, Deyan P. Mihaylov, Glenn D. Starkman, Cynthia Trendafilova, Yashar Akrami, Stefano Anselmi, Javier Carrón Duque, Craig J. Copi, Fernando Cornet-Gomez, Arthur Kosowsky, Anna Negro, Ricardo G. Rodrigues, Andrius Tamosiunas

**Abstract:** The Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence is the expectation value of the log-likelihood ratio between two specified models, evaluated with respect to one of the two models. It provides an average measure of the distinguishability of the models. We examine its connection to discovery potential and the accuracy of parameter estimates when one model represents a null hypothesis, and the alternative is a family of models characterized by one or more continuous parameters. For data drawn from a multivariate Gaussian distribution, we derive the log-likelihood-ratio distribution and its cumulants under two such hypotheses. We then search over the alternative model's parameter space and use peak prominence, the height of a likelihood-profile peak above its surrounding baseline, as an operational detection statistic. Repeating this search on simulated data drawn from the null model allows us to calibrate the prominence threshold for a specified false-positive rate. We study a toy Gaussian model as well as three cosmological examples involving cosmic topology, the tensor-to-scalar ratio, and dark energy. In all these cases, detectability of a particular model depends not only on KL divergence, but also on the size of the model parameter space, the likelihood-ratio distributions, and the distance of the model from any parameter space boundaries. The relationship between KL divergence, discovery potential, and parameter estimation must therefore be calibrated for each family of models and detection statistic under consideration.

**Relevance:** 1
**Novelty:** 4

## 152. [Elastic Properties of Titan Tholins Revealed by Brillouin Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17564) <a id="link152"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17564

**Authors:** Mark A. Johnson, Jose R. Montes-Bojorquez, Adis Husic, Xinting Yu, Wenhao Su, Jia-Sen Hu, Ryan Blase, Edward Patrick, Alan Whittington, Jin S. Zhang

**Abstract:** Titan tholins, complex organic solids synthesized by simulating photochemical processes in Titan's atmosphere, are widely regarded as analogs to organic surface materials on the Saturnian moon. Their mechanical properties are critical for understanding the evolution of Titan's geomorphology and crustal layering. Here, we report the first direct experimental determination of the complete set of aggregate elastic moduli of tholins using Brillouin spectroscopy. Measurements conducted on tholin powder samples yielded a bulk modulus of 11 (2) GPa and a shear modulus of 3.9 (5) GPa, from which we derive a Young's modulus of 11 (1) GPa and a Poisson's ratio of 0.34 (3). Thin-film samples synthesized under slightly different conditions yield similar results. Compared with other Titan-relevant organic compounds, tholins exhibit the highest elastic stiffness measured to date, exceeding even H2O ice in both bulk and shear moduli. These results support the interpretation that complex organics contribute significantly to the mechanical stability and transport behavior of Titan's equatorial dune sediments. Measurements following prolonged atmospheric exposure reveal persistent stiffening of tholins that remains after vacuum treatment, likely associated with minor chemical modification and physical reorganization, highlighting the importance of controlled sample handling for future laboratory investigations. This study also demonstrates the effectiveness of Brillouin spectroscopy as a non-destructive technique for characterizing planetary organic materials and provides new constraints relevant to future seismic and geophysical investigations by the Dragonfly mission.

**Novelty:** 4

## 153. [Structure and evolution of cosmic voids within DESI DR1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18348) <a id="link153"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18348

**Authors:** Óscar Monllor-Berbegal, Susana Planelles, Vicent Quilis

**Abstract:** Ongoing stage IV galaxy surveys such as DESI or Euclid offer an unprecedented amount of information to study the large-scale structure of the Universe by mapping tens of millions of galaxies. Within the context of cosmic voids, already released and future catalogues will help better understand the void structure and evolution as well as the cosmological model constraints derived from their statistics. With the recent DESI Data Release 1, our aim is to obtain a new void catalogue that allows us to study the properties of cosmic voids at different redshifts, hence providing insight not only on their structure but also on their evolution. We select the DESI BGS and LRG samples for this study. Before the void-finding process, the data goes through a preprocessing pipeline tackling redshift space distortions and reconstructing the mildly non-linear velocity field. After that, the new AVISM void finder is applied to the preprocessed data, obtaining two void catalogues in different redshift bins (0.1 < z < 0.3 and 0.4 < z < 0.8), both within a 1400^3 cMpc^3 cube. We successfully recover N_BGS = 1114 and N_LRG = 216 clean voids with sizes up to ~ 65 cMpc. The void size function, 3D shape variables, density profiles, and linear bias are obtained and analysed. Both the size function and the density profiles obtained are in agreement with previous theoretical studies. The expected mass depletion in void evolution is shown by the evolution of density profiles. We obtain a roughly constant linear bias inside r < Re with mean values of 1.31 +/- 0.02 and 2.14 +/- 0.04 for BGS and LRG, respectively. Both void catalogues are publicly released.

## 154. [The compact jets of the black hole X-ray binary Swift J1753.5-0127 during its 2023-2024 outburst](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18687) <a id="link154"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18687

**Authors:** Noa Grollimund, Stéphane Corbel, Lauren Rhodes, David M. Russell, M. Cristina Baglio, Francesco Carotenuto, Tristan Bouchet, Payaswini Saikia, Rob P. Fender, Sara E. Motta, Piergiorgio Casella, Kevin Alabarta, Fraser Lewis, Andrew K. Hughes, Fraser J. Cowie, Justine Crook-Mansour, Xian Zhang

**Abstract:** X-ray binaries are excellent laboratories to study the coupling between accretion and ejection phenomena around compact objects through multiwavelength observations. Swift J1753.5-0127 is a black hole low-mass X-ray binary discovered in outburst in 2005 that remained active for 11 years, followed by two mini-outbursts. We investigate the behavior of the compact jets of Swift J1753.5-0127 during its 2023-2024 outburst. We report a dense multiwavelength monitoring in the radio, NIR/optical/UV and X-ray bands. Using new and archival luminosity measurements, we construct a refined, smoother radio/X-ray plane that covers the steep/outlier and shallow/transition branches of the radio/X-ray correlation. We also present spectral energy distributions (SEDs) constructed using quasi-simultaneous observations from four epochs. Swift J1753.5-0127 underwent a faint, hard-only, double-peaked outburst, followed by a re-brightening in the optical. The updated radio/X-ray plane offers a clear view on the region occupied by this source, similar to MAXI J1348-630, but at lower radio luminosities than H 1743-322, and we confirm its hybrid behavior. The broad-band SED fitting reveals that a cool, truncated accretion disk dominates the NIR/optical/UV band. We find that the compact jets have a variable but weak contribution in the NIR, with a break frequency $ν_\mathrm{break} \lesssim 5 \times 10^{12}~\mathrm{Hz}$, providing additional evidence that radio-quiet sources are also infrared-quiet. We also find evidence that the radio spectral index evolves with X-ray luminosity, transitioning from inverted values near quiescence to flatter or negative values at higher luminosities. In the context of adiabatic jet models, this trend may indicate that the jet geometry becomes increasingly confined as the accretion rate rises.

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18146

**Authors:** Ting Yu, Zhifu Gao, Zhongli Zhang, Weicong Jing

**Abstract:** Context. The Gum Nebula is a nearby ionised complex with sight-line-dependent pulsar propagation. Aims. We test whether a smooth ionised structure can explain low-frequency spectral turnovers and pulse broadening, or whether smaller-scale material is required. Methods. We analysed Murchison Widefield Array observations of six pulsars at 80--240 MHz, combining sub-band flux densities with literature data. We compared power-law, free--free absorption (FFA), and turnover-constrained broken-power-law (BPL) spectra, and modelled scattering through pulse-profile convolution. PSR J0820-4114 and the Vela pulsar were fitted jointly across frequency. Results. All six spectra depart from a single power law. FFA is favoured for PSRs J0737-3039A and J0820-4114, and BPL for J0742-2822 and J0835-4510; the other two classifications depend on assumed error floors. Conditional FFA solutions imply emission measures of $10^4$--$10^5$ pc cm$^{-6}$ for electron temperatures of $3\times10^3$--$10^4$ K, far exceeding smooth-shell expectations. PSRs J0737-3039A and J0837-4135 have scattering laws consistent with $τ_{\rm sc}\proptoν^{-4}$. We obtain $α_{\rm sc}=-3.07\pm0.11$ for J0742-2822 and $-1.59\pm0.44$ for J0820-4114 using a joint two-component model. The Vela pulsar is best described by an effective thick-screen model with $α_{\rm sc}\simeq-2.74$. Neither tested Galactic electron-density model reproduces both independent distances and low-frequency scattering uniformly. Conclusions. The results favour a structured Gum--Vela magneto-ionic environment in which neighbouring sight lines probe different absorbing and scattering material; these effects need not arise in the same plasma component.

## 156. [A Distinct Type of Standstill in Ultra-Compact White Dwarf Binaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19105) <a id="link156"></a>

**Abstract:** AM CVn stars are ultra-compact, semi-detached binaries consisting of a white dwarf primary and a hydrogen-poor secondary. In this work, we analyse the AM CVn-type systems MGAB-V270, ZTF18aaxuusk and ASASSN-19ct, which have similar properties and show unusual post-superoutburst behaviour. We focus on the occurrence of standstill-like phases. In MGAB-V270, superoutbursts are followed by a series of echo outbursts that evolves into a standstill-like phase before the system returns to quiescence. ASASSN-19ct exhibits comparable echo outbursts but no standstill-like phases. Periodicity analysis using TESS data shows that superhumps can persist during both echo outbursts and standstill-like phases. Although standstills have previously been observed in AM CVn stars, the standstill-like phases we observe appear to represent a distinct phenomenon associated with post-superoutburst activity. We compare our results to other AM CVns that may exhibit similar behaviour.

## 157. [Graph Learning with Multiplicative Aggregation for Solar Filament Fragment Association Modeling in Full-disk H$α$ Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17291) <a id="link157"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17291

**Authors:** GaoFei Zhu, GangHua Lin, Xiao Yang, Suo Liu, Yu Liu, ZhiSheng Gao, Cheng Zeng

**Abstract:** Solar filaments are prominent features in H$α$ observations. During their evolution, they often exhibit complex morphological and topological changes, which, due to line-of-sight projection effects, appear as fragmentation and merging. These phenomena make it difficult to reliably track solar filaments using automated methods, as the same structure may appear different over time. To address this issue, we formulate filament fragment association as a graph-based learning problem, where individual fragments are represented as nodes and candidate pairwise relationships are modeled as edges to infer whether fragments belong to the same filament. Furthermore, we propose a novel graph neural network (GNN) that incorporates multiplicative aggregation layers to enhance feature aggregation in the graph, replacing conventional additive aggregation with a multiplicative mechanism to better capture information between fragments. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method achieves reliable performance in filament fragment association, validating the effectiveness of the graph-based formulation and the proposed multiplicative aggregation mechanism.

**Abstract:** We use Gaia Data Release 3 (DR3) light curves to identify photometrically selected changing-state active galactic nucleus (CSAGN) candidates. ## 160. [Finite-Temperature Equations of State for Neutron-Star Mergers within the Quark Mean-Field Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18103) <a id="link160"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18103

**Authors:** Shuochong Han, Xiangdong Sun, Zhenyu Zhu, Ang Li

**Abstract:** We extend the quark mean-field (QMF) model to finite temperature and systematically investigate the thermodynamic properties of uniform nuclear matter, using the relativistic mean-field (RMF) model as a reference. For each model, we consider two parameter sets calibrated to the same selected nuclear-matter saturation properties but with different nucleon Dirac effective-mass ratios at saturation. We examine the effective nucleon mass and the composition of $β$-equilibrium matter, together with the free energy, energy density, pressure, entropy, and heat capacities of symmetric nuclear matter, pure neutron matter, and $β$-equilibrium matter. The QMF and RMF models exhibit similar qualitative trends, but their predictions increasingly differ at high density, where the effective masses have distinct density dependences. These differences are particularly evident in the thermal pressure and thermal index. For the two parameter sets considered in this work, we further provide compact analytical representations of the thermal index, $Γ_{\rm th}(ρ_{\rm b},T,β)$ over baryon densities of 0.08-1.0 $\rm fm^{-3}$, temperatures of 5-100 MeV, and isospin asymmetries of 0-1. The fits reproduce the numerical results with an accuracy better than $2\%$ and can be directly implemented in binary neutron-star merger simulations. This study establishes a finite-temperature extension of the QMF framework and provides a basis for future assessments of how a quark-structure-based description of nuclear matter affects thermal support and post-merger dynamics in binary neutron-star mergers.

## 161. [What Does 2D Coronal Seismology Measure?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17433) <a id="link161"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17433

**Authors:** Zihao Yang, Yuhong Fan, Giuliana de Toma, Sarah Gibson, Matthias Rempel

**Abstract:** Two-dimensional (2D) coronal seismology based on coronagraphic imaging spectroscopy has emerged as a promising method for mapping the coronal magnetic field, but the physical meaning of its inferred quantities under optically thin line-of-sight (LOS) integration remains uncertain. We investigate this problem by synthesizing coronagraphic observables from a global wave-driven magnetohydrodynamic model with a long LOS extent and a structured corona, and by applying the same analysis procedures used for observations. We compare the diagnosed density, wave propagation direction, phase speed, and magnetic-field strength with different LOS-weighted reference quantities from the model. We find that the measured density and the wave propagation direction are both well represented by LOS emissivity-weighted quantities. In contrast, the phase speed is not controlled by emissivity weighting alone: the LOS distribution of wave velocity amplitude also plays an important role. As a result, the inferred magnetic field is better represented by combining the emissivity-weighted density with an amplitude-dependent effective speed. Emissivity weighting nevertheless remains a reasonable approximation, and a useful baseline, over much of the field of view, especially where the LOS does not cross strongly structured plasma-sheet-like regions. These results clarify the physical interpretation of 2D coronal seismology and provide guidance for future coronal magnetic-field tomography and model-constrained applications.

**Abstract:** We studied the detector response for a liquid-argon time-projection chamber (LArTPC) Compton camera targeting MeV gamma-ray astronomy by a Geant4-based Monte Carlo simulation. We evaluated the angular resolution measure (ARM) and the energy reconstruction error as functions of electronic noise, pixel pitch, and the recombination correction method. An electronic noise level of 4 keV and a pixel pitch of 1 mm yield an ARM (FWHM) of about 3 degrees at 1332.5 keV and an energy reconstruction error better than 4\% for incident energies of 1173.2 keV and above. Recombination correction using both scintillation light and charge signals improves the ARM and the energy error by factors of 1.7--2.3 and 2.2--5.4, respectively, compared to the charge-only correction, although the uncertainty of the recombination-fluctuation model remains. Since this simulation relies on idealized assumptions such as the use of the Monte Carlo truth in the event reconstruction, these values represent the best cases achievable under these detector conditions; nevertheless, they show that a large-scale LArTPC is a promising Compton camera for cosmic MeV gamma-ray observations.

For the harder candidate {neutrino} spectrum of NGC~7469, the adopted model yields more energetic nuclear cascades, while the source-specific flux selections allow larger nuclear fractions for the heavier mixtures than in NGC~1068. ## 164. [Coherent states of neutrinos in rotating matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19078) <a id="link164"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19078

**Authors:** Alexander Breev, Maxim Dvornikov

**Abstract:** We study coherent states of neutrinos electroweakly interacting with rotating matter. We start with the Dirac equation for a massive neutrino propagating in background matter with a coordinate dependent effective potential. Then, we consider the limit of massless particles and the specific matter velocity profile. It allows us to reduce the neutrino Hamiltonian to the harmonic oscillator form. We find the exact solutions of the Dirac equation and construct the creation and annihilation operators. Using these results, we build the generalized coherent states of neutrinos. We provide the interpretation of these coherent states in terms of the classical physics. In particular, we demonstrate that the coherent states constructed approach plane waves in the limit of slow matter rotation. We also define the effective neutrino mass owing to the interaction of a massless neutrino with rotating matter. Then, we consider a special case of the generalized coherent states which are called the neutrino coherent states. The situation of the degenerate parameters of the system is also studied. Finally, we apply our results for astrophysical neutrinos propagating in dense matter of a rapidly rotating neutron star (NS). We calculate the effective neutrino masses for different neutrino energies for realistic parameters of NS.

## 165. [Radiation limits on amino acid preservation and chiral measurement at Oxia Planum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17931) <a id="link165"></a>

**Authors:** Dimitra Atri

**Abstract:** Amino acid enantiomeric excess (EE) is a potential biosignature, but its measurement requires sufficient surviving material as well as preserved chirality. We derive a criterion that separates abundance-limited from racemization-limited preservation and apply it to the Mars Organic Molecule Analyzer (MOMA) on the ExoMars Rosalind Franklin rover. The model combines cosmic-ray and radiogenic doses with laboratory destruction kinetics and assumed ratios of interconversion to destruction rates. For an initially homochiral population of 1000~ppmw, the surviving EE is 58\% or 25\% when the total abundance reaches 1~ppmw for rate ratios of 0.04 or 0.10, respectively. This abundance criterion does not ensure that both enantiomers can be quantified. Applying provisional estimates of 1~ppmw per enantiomer and 10\% EE, serine at 2~m requires initial concentrations of 340 and 960~ppmw after cosmic-ray exposures of $10^{7}$ and $10^{8}$~yr, respectively, under transferred fused-silica--perchlorate kinetics and a 1.57-MGy radiogenic dose. These concentrations exceed measured meteoritic serine abundances, but do not predict the initial Oxia inventory. For organics retained in a closed host since deposition 3.9~Ga ago, the modeled radiogenic dose is 1.6--5.6~MGy across the adopted compositions. In the lower-dose case, drilling to 2~m reduces the total dose by only a factor of 1.5 after $10^{7}$~yr. The boundaries depend on mineral kinetics and organic residence time; they distinguish preserved chirality from a measurable excess.

## 167. [R(V) parameter of the dust extinction curve: mapping based on hot stars and connections to diffuse interstellar bands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17051) <a id="link167"></a>

**Authors:** R. Lallement, J. L. Vergely, N. L. J. Cox

**Abstract:** Favored carriers of the Diffuse Interstellar Bands (DIBs) are carbonaceous molecules whose constituents have been presumably expelled by dying stars along with those of dust grains. We inverted a catalog of extinction data based on Gaia XP spectra and NIR photometry, and used the resulting new 3D extinction map to locate the opaque areas responsible for R(V) for each star. Using archived spectra, we measured their 5780, 5797 and 8620 A DIBs. In parallel, we compared local and line-of-sight (LOS) R(V)s from recent 3D maps with local and LOS Gaia DIB strengths from our recent map. Results: R(V)s from hot stars reveal variations on large spatial scales, confirming with independent data the published 3D pattern. Gaia DIB strength maps show large spatial scale variations similar to those of R(V) maps, with higher abundance of DIB carriers corresponding to higher R(V). LOS Gaia DIB strengths and R(V)s from 3D maps also correlate positively in the R(V) range 2.3-3.4, but there is no connection for higher R(V), likely due to the disappearance of DIB carriers in dense clouds. Despite averaging effects along the LOS to hot stars, we find differences in R(V)-DIB relationships among the three DIBs, with an evolution from negative to positive correlation from the zeta- to the sigma-type DIBs. This can be understood in the frame of the bimodal evolution of R(V) with the extinction, with the zeta (resp. sigma) DIB carrier disappearance in the decreasing (resp. increasing) phase of R(V). Various combinations of DIB strengths and DIB ratios may allow to define the range of R(V), but the relationship between DIBs and R(V) also appears to depend on the wavelength range used to determine R(V).

## 168. [An end-to-end experimental pipeline for automated track identification for paleo-detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17008) <a id="link168"></a>

**Authors:** Claudio Galelli, Alessandro Testi, Lorenzo Caccianiga

**Abstract:** Paleo-detectors aim to exploit the damage tracks left in natural minerals by cosmic messengers over geological times to reconstruct past fluxes of energetic particles. For cosmic rays, the expected signal is abundant, and the tracks are micron-scale, so they can be revealed by etching and read out with conventional optical microscopy; what remains challenging is turning a bulk sample into a calibrated track-length spectrum that can be quantitatively confronted with theoretical predictions. In this work we present the experimental pipeline we developed for this purpose within the INFN PRImuS experiment, from sample preparation and automated multi-focus microscopy to track recognition with OptimusPrimus, a two-stage machine-learning algorithm, and to a forward-folding model that propagates geometric sectioning, etching and the measured detection response into simulated spectra. The pipeline was built and validated on chemically etched obsidian where the recovered track-length distribution is statistically compatible with expert annotation. In parallel, we developed and characterized a dry argon-plasma etching protocol for halite, an evaporite mineral of particular interest as a low-background cosmic-ray paleo-detector. Since every stage of the pipeline is mineral-agnostic, with only the trained networks and the detection response to be re-derived from annotated data, we are now applying it to plasma-etched halite with the aim of identifying cosmic-ray-induced tracks.

## 169. [Experimental evidence for physical alteration of fine regolith on near-Sun asteroids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17652) <a id="link169"></a>

**Abstract:** The surfaces of airless bodies evolve through processes such as (micro)meteoroid impacts and thermal fatigue, which modify physical properties such as grain size, porosity, and mineral texture. These properties provide clues to surface evolution and are necessary for interpreting telescopic observations. Near-Sun asteroids (NSAs), which reach heliocentric distances of $\lesssim$0.2 au, are notable targets, as their surfaces are likely modified by intense solar radiation. However, experimental studies of how radiative heating modifies the physical properties of carbonaceous asteroid surfaces remain scarce compared with studies of thermally induced compositional and spectral changes. We investigate these modifications experimentally. Near-Sun conditions at heliocentric distances $0.09<r<0.19$ au are simulated using the Space and High-Irradiance Near-Sun Simulator. CM-type carbonaceous chondrite simulants are irradiated under vacuum. Brightness is monitored in situ with a video camera during heating, and morphological changes are examined using a stage microscope and a scanning electron microscope (SEM). The irradiated samples show initial darkening followed by brightening, consolidation, and morphological modifications from sub-micrometer to millimeter scales. The observed brightness modifications are more consistent with changes in the absorption properties of the sample than with variations in surface roughness. SEM imaging of the heated samples shows structures similar to those formed by sintering, which are completely absent in unheated samples. These results suggest that sintering is a likely candidate for the mechanism responsible for the observed consolidation and morphological changes. This work provides experimental evidence to help constrain the surface evolution of NSAs in general, and supports sintering as a possible mechanism proposed for (3200) Phaethon in particular.

## 170. [Integration of a CME Flux Rope Model with Magnetic Field Draping in Heliospheric Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16004) <a id="link170"></a>

**Authors:** Ranadeep Sarkar, Jens Pomoell, Emilia Kilpua, Eleanna Asvestari

**Abstract:** Accurate prediction of the magnetic structure of interplanetary coronal mass ejections (ICMEs) remains a key challenge in space weather forecasting. Despite several efforts using global MHD modeling, it remains difficult to deduce a flux-rope solution that can accurately and reliably reproduce the magnetic structure of a CME. We present FROST (Flux Rope embedded in the Solar Magnetic Field), a new flux-rope model implementation within the European Heliospheric Forecast Information Asset (EUHFORIA), featuring an initially force-free toroidal flux rope embedded in the low-coronal magnetic field. FROST employs a novel embedding technique that self-consistently generates a draping magnetic field around the flux-rope, ensuring continuity of the normal magnetic field component at the CME boundary. The dynamics of the flux-rope in the low and middle corona is solved by a non-uniform advection constrained by the observed kinematics of the event. This results in a global, non-toroidal, stretched loop-like magnetic structure. At heliospheric distances, the evolution is modeled as an MHD process using EUHFORIA. We validate the FROST model implementation by simulating the CME event of 2013 April 11 and comparing the results with in situ magnetic field measurements at 1 au. The modeled magnetic field profiles of the flux-rope exhibit strong agreement with the observations, with correlation coefficients ranging from 0.64-0.93 for Bx, 0.92-0.95 for By, and 0.82-0.87 for Bz component of the magnetic field. These results demonstrate the capability of the proposed flux-rope model to significantly improve ICME magnetic field prediction, highlighting its strong potential for advancing space-weather forecasting capabilities.

**Abstract:** It has recently been demonstrated that the occurrence of extreme solar events is governed by a phase-stable period close to 1.723 years (629.29 days). Overall, the squared sum of these three waves incorporates nine periods ranging from 59 to 629 days. Using a standard Rayleigh test, we demonstrate that all nine of these periods can be identified in the data on extreme solar events. For the latter, we use a combination of 72 ground-level enhancement (GLE) events with various X-flare data, the number of which increases when the threshold is successively lowered from X10 to X1. To identify the spectral peaks, it is necessary to analyze the sidebands that emerge due to the modulation with the longer-term periods such as the 11-year Schwabe cycle and the third harmonics of the Hale cycle. In this framework, the seminal Rieger period of 154 days is re-interpreted as the lower sideband of 146 days which is the quarter of the synodic period of Venus and Earth.

Because our transfer matrices are singular, inversion relies on singular value decom- position regularization. Ground-based observations lose the equatorial North-South anisotropy component, severely degrading interstellar map recovery. We developed a geometric circle-fitting algorithm to estimate and reintroduce the missing component into the observed signals. Our results show that restoring the North-South anisotropy en- ables more accurate recovery of the interstellar dipole structure. Furthermore, Poisson statistical fluctuations on the detected event counts dominate matrix-transformation errors when spread over multiple energies.

## 173. [Velocity-field estimation techniques for on-disk solar coronal bright fronts (CBFs) and shock-wave propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19032) <a id="link173"></a>

**Authors:** Oleg Stepanyuk, Kamen Kozarev

**Abstract:** Coronal mass ejections (CMEs) are large expulsions of plasma and magnetic fields from the solar corona into the heliosphere. Coronal shock waves are driven by CMEs, and accurate Solar Energetic Particles (SEP) forecasting requires knowing when and where shocks form, how fast they propagate, and how they interact with the coronal magnetic field. By measuring the speed, geometry and temporal evolution of coronal bright fronts (CBFs), one can create more realistic predictions of SEP onset and intensity at Earth, thus velocity-field estimation techniques supply these critical parameters. High-resolution velocity field maps can provide even deeper insight. Instead of yielding only an overall speed of the front, techniques like FLCT and optical flow can show the spatial distribution of velocities across the entire wave dome or front. These analyses reveal which parts of the front are moving fastest and how the front might be deforming as it expands. Here we discuss a set of practical parameters for Lucas--Kanade, Horn--Schunck, FLCT, and Farneback algorithms, arrived at by combining analytic reasoning with empirical measurements on a single event---and demonstrate the potential of these techniques for on-disk shock-wave propagation during the X1.5-class event that took place 10 May 2022, processed by the wavelet-based Wavetrack technique. A better understanding of how to fine-tune these parameters could improve the precision of algorithms running in pipelines, and is especially valuable where per-event hand-tuning is impractical.

## 174. [TESS flares and chromospheric variability in the Pleiades, the Hyades, and Praesepe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16772) <a id="link174"></a>

**Authors:** A. Görgei, K. Vida, B. Seli, K. Oláh, P. Sági, B. Schmercz, R. M. Roettenbacher, Zs. Kővári

**Abstract:** Magnetic activity is a result of the interaction between rotation and dynamo processes in cool stars with outer convection zones. Since open cluster members share a common age, distance, and chemical composition, they provide an opportunity to study the magnetic activity of cool stars in a controlled astrophysical context. We used 120s cadence observations from the Transiting Exoplanet Survey Satellite (TESS) to study the flare activity of stars in three open clusters with different ages: the Pleiades (100 Myr), the Hyades (700 Myr) and Praesepe (700 Myr). We used the Ohio State Multi-Object Spectrograph (OSMOS) at MDM Observatory with the goal of identifying coronal mass ejections (CMEs) in the Pleiades and Praesepe. We investigated the flaring characteristics of the cluster members using the TESS light curves: we identified the flares, then divided the dataset into different categories based on the effective temperature and light curve amplitude. We calculated the flare energies and investigated the flare frequency distributions. We studied the dynamic spectra of the H$α$ line and its environment to search for indication of CMEs. The power-law indices of the flare frequency distributions obtained in our analysis span the 1.8-2.8 range. We found the highest flare rates in the Pleiades for all investigated spectral types. Our results show that the flare rate of M dwarfs is similar in the Pleiades and the Hyades while for K-type stars the flare rate in the Pleiades is an order of magnitude higher. Our analysis reveals that the flare rate in M stars changes with their effective temperature: the rate is highest for T$_{eff}$ $\sim$3200-3300 K -coinciding with the transition between partially and fully convective stars - with smooth decrease in flare rate towards cooler and hotter M dwarfs. We used the MDM/OSMOS spectra to place an upper limit on the CME rate.

**Novelty:** 4

## 175. [Science-Driven Performance Requirements for UV Coronagraph Design: Implications for O$_3$ Detection in Earth-like Atmospheres](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19081) <a id="link175"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.19081

**Authors:** Amber V. Young, Giada Arney, Shawn Domagal-Goldman, Tyler D. Robinson, Roser Juanola-Parramon, Christopher Stark, Renyu Hu, Armen Tokadjian, Mario Damiano

**Abstract:** Detecting biosignatures on Earth-like exoplanets from the Proterozoic era requires instrumentation capable of probing near-ultraviolet (NUV) spectral features, particularly ozone (\ce{O3}), which serves as an indirect proxy for molecular oxygen (\ce{O2}) when direct detection is challenging. Using a combination of photochemical-climate modeling and atmospheric retrieval simulations, we quantify fiducial instrument performance requirements for robust \ce{O3} detection and assess the risk of spectral false positives from sulfur dioxide (\ce{SO2}). Our analysis demonstrates the \ce{O3} abundances for Proterozoic Earth-like planets with \ce{O2} as low as 0.1\% the present atmospheric level, can be constrained to within an order of magnitude at 1\,$σ$ confidence using three adjacent photometric bandpasses at a signal-to-noise ratio of 10 at 0.366\,$\upmu$m, each with a 15\% width, and a short wavelength cutoff for those observations near 0.26\,$\upmu$m. The self-consistent steady state atmospheric modeling results show that \ce{SO2} buildup is difficult in a habitable environment with appreciable amounts of water vapor in its atmosphere and would require volcanic outgassing rates at least two orders of magnitude above present-day Earth to reach surface abundance levels comparable to Proterozoic Earth-like abundances of \ce{O3}. Even under these high \ce{SO2} flux scenarios, \ce{SO2} remains largely unconstrained with the recommended observational configuration, reinforcing the need for NUV coverage in coronagraphic instruments. Our findings support the inclusion of UV-optimized optics and detectors on the Habitable Worlds Observatory (HWO) to enable biosignature detection strategies sensitive to atmospheric conditions across key phases of Earth's geological history.

## 176. [Sulphur-bearing molecule survey towards prestellar and starless cores in the Perseus molecular cloud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17556) <a id="link176"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17556

**Authors:** J. Ferrer Asensio, S. Scibelli, A. Pokorny-Yadav, L. Steffes, B. Kulterer, Y. Shirley, A. Megías, I. Jiménez-Serra, A. Fuente, G. Esplugues

**Abstract:** Sulphur is drastically depleted from the gas phase in dense starless and prestellar cores, creating the long-standing "missing sulphur problem". Observations of sulphur-bearing molecules are crucial to understanding sulphur chemistry, core-environment relationships, and the physical evolution of star-forming regions. We present a survey of four sulphur-bearing molecules, HCS+, OCS, H2CS, and SO, towards 15 starless and prestellar cores in the Perseus molecular cloud using the Yebes 40m telescope. The HCS+ 1-0, OCS 3-2 and 4-3, H2CS 1_{0,1}-0_{0,0}, and SO 3_2-2_2 transitions were observed, fitted with Gaussian profiles, and column densities were calculated assuming local thermodynamic equilibrium (LTE). We also compare these with observations of CS, CCS, and CCCS from a previous study. HCS+, OCS, H2CS, and SO are detected in 100%, 93%, 100%, and 73% of the sample, respectively. Derived column densities are (2.2-17.0)x10^11 cm^-2 for HCS+, (1.9-25.0)x10^12 cm^-2 for OCS, (1.0-15.0)x10^12 cm^-2 for H2CS, and (0.3-17.0)x10^13 cm^-2 for SO, spanning over an order of magnitude. HCS+ and SO abundances strongly correlate (inversely and directly, respectively) with core volume density, reinforcing their early- and late-type status. Correlations indicate a differentiation between carbon and oxygen chemistry for sulphur-bearing molecules, with CS showing the strongest correlations as expected. Cores in protostellar outflow paths do not show consistently higher abundances. The chemical age (SO/CS) correlates with physical age (volume density), except for Per752, an outlier likely linked to an enhanced cosmic ray ionization rate. These results provide a robust observational baseline constraining chemical models of sulphur chemistry in early star formation.

## 177. [The XMM Cluster Survey: Cross-Calibration of eROSITA and XMM using Dark Energy Survey Y3 Galaxy Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16741) <a id="link177"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16741

**Authors:** J. E. Pilling, D. J. Turner, P. A. Giles, A. K. Romer, R. Wilkinson, P. J. Rooney, M. Hilton, S. Bhargava, J. Hall, R. Wang, A. Abdulghafour, B. J. Maughan

**Abstract:** We present the cross-calibration of the eROSITA and XMM telescopes, using galaxy clusters detected by redMaPPer from the first three years of Dark Energy Survey observations (DESY3RM). We use the X-ray: Generate and Analyse (XGA) Python module to measure eROSITA properties, and validate our methodology using a deep pointed eROSITA CAL-PV observation of A3158, and a sample of 119 clusters from the expanded HIgh FLux Galaxy Cluster Sample (eeHiFLUGCS). Next, we construct a sample of DESY3RM clusters matched to XMM observations analysed by the XMM Cluster Survey (XCS), and to clusters in the first release of the eROSITA All-Sky Survey (eRASS1). This produced a sample of 114 DESY3RM clusters with both XMM and eROSITA detections. Our resulting X-ray temperatures determined from eROSITA were compared to eRASS1 catalogue values, showing consistency between the two analyses. Comparisons between XMM and eROSITA temperatures reveal a systematic offset, with XMM temperatures 28$\pm$7% higher on average. Finally, we fit $L_{\rm X,soft}$-$λ_{\rm RM}$ and $T_{\rm X}$-$λ_{\rm RM}$ scaling relations for both eROSITA and XMM using the same cluster sample, finding minimal differences in the $L_{\rm X,soft}$-$λ_{\rm RM}$ relation but a clear normalisation offset in the $T_{\rm X}$-$λ_{\rm RM}$ relation, particularly for the highest richness systems. This study will enable future work combining eROSITA and XMM observatories for DESY3RM clusters.

## 178. [Simulation and Deep Learning Inversion for Full-sky Energetic Neutral Atom Imaging at the Heliospheric Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17414) <a id="link178"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17414

**Authors:** Weining Wang, Rong Lin, Jiansen He, Hong Zou, Yongfu Wang, Jiali Chen, Yuguang Ye, Weihong Shi, Wei Zhang, Xi Lu, Die Duan, Tianhang Chen, Linghua Wang, Qiugang Zong

**Abstract:** The distribution of energetic neutral atom (ENA) differential flux density at the heliospheric boundary is the key to revealing the interaction mechanism between the solar wind and the interstellar medium. The Helios ENA Rapid Mapper (HERMA) is a pre-research project for a new ENA detection concept and technical principle, which is designed to provide technical support for refined detection with its advantages of wide energy range coverage and high spatial resolution. To verify the high spatiotemporal resolution rapid imaging capability of the HERMA, this study restored the particle response characteristics of the HERMA (spatial modulation grid + 256-pixel linear array silicon detector) based on Geant4 simulation, and we compared the performance of the traditional inversion algorithm with the convolutional neural network (CNN) inversion algorithm using INCA data as the benchmark. The results show that under different sampling times, the source distribution inverted by the CNN algorithm has a higher degree of agreement with the ideally expected distribution, and the CNN algorithm exhibits better stability and accuracy than the traditional algorithm under the conditions of low signal-to-noise ratio and short staring time. This study provides core technical support for the engineering verification and on-orbit application of the HERMA instrument, fills the technical gap in wide-energy-range and high-resolution ENA detection, and helps to deepen the research on the interaction mechanism between the solar wind and the interstellar medium at the heliospheric boundary.

## 179. [Henrietta Leavitt's classical Cepheid relation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17415) <a id="link179"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17415

**Authors:** Daniel Majaess

**Abstract:** The Leavitt & Pickering (1912) mean period-magnitude relation for SMC Cepheids exhibits correlated residuals relative to modern Gaia $B_G$ and Johnson $V$ photometry ($r_{δV/δL12}\approx0.76$). Therefore dispersion inherent to Leavitt's photographic photometry relation is not solely random scatter ($σ_{L12,B_G,V}=0.22\pm0.03,0.25\pm0.04,0.20\pm0^{m}.03$), and is potentially consistent with the partial encoding of combined effects from the elongated SMC, classical Cepheid instability strip, differential reddening, etc. OGLE data indicate that $\gtrsim870$ SMC variables identified by Leavitt (1908, Table 2) are classical Cepheids ($\gtrsim 90$ %), and the preliminary unphased Table 2 photometry likewise features correlated residuals relative to contemporary observations ($r_{δB_G/δL08-T2}\approx0.54$). Table 6 data in Leavitt (1908, $n=16$) define a highly linear Leavitt law over an extensive baseline ($r\approx-0.98$), but constitute a smaller sample than the later 1912 work ($n=25$).

## 180. [Revisiting the Role of Global Magnetic Variables in Coronal Heating of Solar Active Regions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17972) <a id="link180"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17972

**Authors:** David H. Brooks, Harry P. Warren, Johnathan R. Stauffer

**Abstract:** Reconnection-based models of coronal heating predict that magnetic complexity---non-potential configurations, currents, shear---should enhance coronal emission independently of total magnetic flux. Previous work, however, found no evidence that complexity contributes independently to coronal heating (Fisher et al. 1998). We revisit this question using SDO/HMI vector magnetograms and SDO/AIA EUV imaging. HMI Space-weather Active Region Patches (SHARPs) provide 16 magnetic parameters characterizing field strength, gradients, currents, helicity, and non-potentiality. We measure spatially-integrated transition region and coronal emission using AIA 304A, 193A, 94A, and isolated Fe XVIII emission for 137 active regions, sampling plasma at ~0.09MK, ~1.4MK, and ~7.1MK. Partial correlation analysis isolates the contribution of each complexity parameter after controlling for total unsigned vertical magnetic flux. Unsigned flux (Phi_M) explains 72--82\% of variance in emission, following power laws (I ~ Phi_M^alpha, where alpha=0.61-1.19) consistent with prior soft X-ray results. Partial correlations reveal weak positive contributions (r = 0.2-0.3) to transition region (304A) emission from some vertical current and field parameters, but little evidence that other active-region-averaged measures of magnetic complexity enhance coronal emission. The exception is total photospheric magnetic free energy density (rho_tot), which shows a positive partial correlation (r = 0.49, p < 0.001) with Fe XVIII emission, but negative, or non-existent, partial correlations with other AIA filters. These results suggest quasi-steady coronal heating scales with magnetic flux, while rho_tot may contribute to high temperature coronal heating. At lower temperatures, regions with more stored energy than expected for their flux produce less EUV emission than expected.

**Relevance:** 1
**Novelty:** 3

## 181. [EMU detection of mysterious multiple component radio-continuum emission from nearby spiral Seyfert I type galaxy 'Sand-timer' (NGC 7213)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18285) <a id="link181"></a>

L. Payne, S. F. ur Rahman, G. P. Rowell, H. Sano, M. Selby, S. S. Shabala, E. J. Stanton, S. Taziaux, K. Tsuge, D. Urosević, N. F. H. Tothill, J. Th. van Loon, V. Velović, G. L. White

**Abstract:** We present an Australian Square Kilometre Array Pathfinder (ASKAP) Evolutionary Map of the Universe (EMU) radio-continuum detection of the nearby spiral Seyfert I type galaxy NGC 7213 which we nickname `Sand-timer' due to its morphological appearance. We find intriguing, peculiar emission extending from the central supermassive black hole (SMBH), which we refer to as galaxy hyperbubbles and/or active galactic nucleus (AGN) jets. Finally, we find two previously undetected kiloparsec-scale features in the polarised intensity image, which appear most likely also related to the central SMBH, as restarted bipolar radio jets, though somewhat obscured in the radio-continuum total intensity image. They resemble the bow shocks often seen in Fanaroff-Riley Class II (FR-II) radio galaxies, and so we propose they most likely represent an early episode of the `Sand-timer's' AGN jet activity. An alternative scenario is that the core of NGC 7213 may be a supermassive binary black hole (SMBBH) undergoing a tidal disruption event (TDE), potentially causing the peculiar radio-continuum emission.

Aljamal, A. E. Evrard, E. W. Upsdell, P. T. P. Viana, A. Abdulghafour, M. Aguena, S. S. Allam, O. Alves, F. Andrade-Oliveira, D. Bacon, D. Brooks, R. Camilleri, A. Carnero Rosell, J. Carretero, M. Costanzi, L. N. da Costa, J. De Vicente, S. Desai, P. Doel, C. Doux, B. Flaugher, J. Frieman, J. Garc, M. Gatti, D. Gruen, G. Gutierrez, S. R. Hinton, D. L. Hollowood, K. Honscheid, K. Kuehn, O. Lahav, S. Lee, J. L. Marshall, J. Mena-Fernández, F. Menanteau, R. Miquel, R. C. Nichol, R. L. C. Ogando, A. A. Plazas Malagón, S. Samuroff, E. Sanchez, D. Sanchez Cid, M. Smith, M. E. da Silva Pereira, M. Soares-Santos, E. Suchyta, M. E. C. Swanson, C. To, D. L. Tucker, V. Vikram, A. R. Walker, N. Weaverdyck, J. Weller

**Abstract:** We present the cross-match of galaxy clusters detected by redMaPPer using the first three years of Dark Energy Survey (DESY3RM) operations, and the first release of the eROSITA-All Sky Survey (eRASS1) taken by eROSITA on board the Spectrum Roentgen Gamma telescope. We define a sky region ($60^{\circ}<{\rm R.A.}<90^{\circ}$ and $-58^{\circ}<{\rm Dec}<-48^{\circ}$) whereby the mean eRASS1 exposure time of clusters falling within this footprint is $\sim$2.5 times longer than the mean exposure time of the full eRASS1--DESY3RM overlap. Limiting to a redshift range $0.2 < z < 0.65$ and redMaPPer estimated richnesses to $>$50, we obtain a sample of 57 clusters. We match these clusters to the cluster catalogue constructed for the eRASS1, finding 45 clusters with a match and 12 with no match in the catalogue. X-ray properties of the 12 unmatched clusters are estimated via a forced photometry analysis. We investigate the form of the luminosity-richness relation, finding the relation using the full 57 cluster sample is statistically consistent with using only the 45 matched clusters, and will require the deeper data of future eRASS releases (or dedicated X-ray follow-up) to explore further potential differences. Using external data from XMM, ACT and SPT, we find detections of 5 of the 12 unmatched clusters, indicating the presence of virialised gas. Finally, we found the 12 unmatched clusters occupy a higher redshift range and lower richness range (compared to the matched clusters), and find no evidence for an underlying physical reason for the lack of X-ray emission.

## 183. [Institutional broadening of the Japanese astronomy and astrophysics research community during 1996-2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16633) <a id="link183"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16633

**Authors:** Hideaki Fujiwara

**Abstract:** Using bibliometric data from publication records, we investigate the institutional broadening of Japanese astronomy and astrophysics over 1996-2025. The number of Japan-affiliated publications increased from 588 to 2,156, and the number of unique participating Japanese institutions increased from 123 to 227, indicating a nominal broadening of the institutional base. However, institution-publication links remained concentrated among leading institutions, and the effective number of institutions, a frequency-weighted diversity measure, increased more gradually than the number of unique participating institutions. These results show that Japanese astronomy and astrophysics broadened institutionally, but not through a complete equalization of institutional participation. The observed change is best interpreted as an expansion of the participating institutional base around a persistent core of leading institutions.

## 184. [Simulating Ionospheric Distortions in Mock SKA-Low Solar Radio Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18080) <a id="link184"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18080

**Authors:** Rohit Sharma, Mitin

**Abstract:** The Square Kilometre Array Observatory (SKAO) will provide unprecedented angular resolution for low-frequency solar radio imaging, enabling access to fine-scale coronal structures. However, the improved spatial resolution of SKA-Low also increases the sensitivity of solar images to ionospheric propagation effects. We investigate the impact of ionospheric fluctuations on simulated SKA-Low solar observations at 50, 100, 150, and 200 MHz using the \textsc{Karabo} simulator and turbulent, moving ionospheric phase screens. We quantify the resulting image distortions using spatial power spectral density (PSD) analysis and compare the SKA response with corresponding MWA simulations. The results show that ionospheric distortions are strongly scale- and frequency-dependent, with the maximum fractional PSD distortion decreasing with observing frequencies. SKA resolves ionospheric fluctuations over spatial scales that are largely smoothed by the MWA instrumental response, with significant distortions occurring on scales comparable to solar active-region structures. These results demonstrate that ionospheric propagation effects should be considered when interpreting high-resolution SKA-low solar radio images, particularly at the lower end of the frequency band.

**Novelty:** 3

## 185. [Initial Performance Tests of MASTA for Wide-Field Narrowband Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16903) <a id="link185"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16903

**Authors:** Mingxuan Xu, Jiang-Tao Li, Li Ji, Chen Zhang, Yantian Zhu

**Abstract:** Wide-field narrowband imaging provides an efficient approach for mapping low-surface-brightness line emission over large angular scales. We present initial performance tests of Multi-Application Survey Telescope Array (MASTA) for narrowband imaging. Using commissioning data obtained with the QHY461 and COSMOS-8K cameras, we characterize the basic reduction procedure, photometric calibration, image quality, and point-source detection limits. The measured surface-brightness and point-source detection limits become deeper with increasing exposure time, roughly following the expected background-limited scaling. Under the current observing and reduction conditions, the COSMOS-8K camera generally reaches deeper surface-brightness limits, particularly in the red filters, while QHY461 has smaller pixel size and provides better sampling of the telescope point spread function (PSF). As demonstrations, we perform continuum subtraction for the nearby galaxy M106 and the supernova remnant Cygnus Loop using adjacent narrowband filters and reveal the Hα and [O III] structures in M106 and filamentary structures in the Cygnus Loop. We further compare the MASTA surface brightness Hα map of M106 with an independent SITELLE measurement. After accounting for [N II] contamination and the wavelength-dependent response of the 6544 Å filter in the f /1.7 converging beam, the corrected median ratio is 0.973. These tests indicate that MASTA is a promising facility for wide-field narrowband imaging. Further improvements in calibration frames, including flux calibration, field-dependent bandpass characterization, and spatially varying PSF modeling and matching will be required for quantitative studies of diffuse ionized gas.

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.18091

**Authors:** Rohit Sharma, Yogesh Wadadekar

**Abstract:** The SKA Regional Centre Network (SRCNet) is a federated global network designed to archive, distribute, and process SKAO data products. This paper examines the evolving SRCNet architecture-focusing on the transition to version 0.2 (v0.2) requirements, including computation, orchestration, and multi-tiered storage-and details the strategy for establishing its proto-SRC infrastructure. We outline the prototype developments for the major and minor nodes in the context of v0.2. We describe the minor node compute and storage demands, establishing a pathway toward a fully operational Indian SKA Regional Centre (INSRC).

# Paper Selection Prompt

## System Prompt

> You are a helpful paper reading assistant whose job is to read daily posts from ArXiv and rank the papers in order according to your friend's interests.
> Your job is to carefully read the paper titles and abstracts below and determine how well they match the criteria below.

## User Prompt

> ## Instructions
> 
> Write the response in JSONL format with {ARXIVID, COMMENT, RELEVANCE, NOVELTY} on each line, one for each paper.
> 
> - ARXIVID: should be the ArXiv ID.
> - COMMENT: for papers with RELEVANCE 7-10, briefly identify the specific matching criterion in at most 10 words. For papers with RELEVANCE 1-6, use an empty string. Never explain why a paper is irrelevant.
> - RELEVANCE: should be a score from 1-10.
> - NOVELTY: should be a score from 1-10.
> 
> 
> ## Scoring Criteria
> 
> > The "Relevance" score measures how closely the paper aligns with the core topics of the prompt.
> > The "Novelty" score assesses the originality and impact of the paper.
> > They are two **ORTHONORMAL** axes and **SHOULD NOT** be confused with each other.
> > When determining the final ranking of papers, the relevance score should be given twice as much weight as the novelty score.
> 
> ### Relevance Scoring
> 
> - Relevance 9-10 (Completely Relevant)
>   - Focus: Fully aligned with core topics with no deviation, score the highest if contains relevant keywords in it.
>   - Examples: Papers focused on foundational methods or theoretical research whose topic directly relates to core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 7-8 (Relevant)
>   - Focus: Retain a solid link to the main research area, though may touch on peripheral elements.
>   - Examples: Papers whose topics generally align with the core topics, but may other aspects or applications not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 5-6 (Borderline)
>   - Focus: Maintains a link to the core topics but also extends into at least one other domain/area beyond the primary focus.
>   - Examples: Papers whose results have implications for the core topics but are not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 3-4 (Irrelevant)
>   - Focus: Largely outside our interests with only very weak association to our topics.
>   - Examples: Papers whose results may have limited impact on the core topics and are not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 1-2 (Ignore)
>   - Focus: Purely unrelated to our topics. Completely a different domain.
> 
> ### Novelty Scoring
> 
> - Novelty 9-10 (Breakthrough)
>   - Definition: Groundbreaking methods/theory/application introducing new directions or solving major challenges.
>   - Examples: Entirely new paradigm for core topics.
> 
> - Novelty 7-8 (Improvements)
>   - Definition: Substantial insights/enhancements, though not a full paradigm shift.
>   - Examples: Modifications on existing methods yielding significantly better results. Applications to new datasets that directly address the core topics. Re-analysis of old datasets using new methods that directly address the core topics.
> 
> - Novelty 5-6 (Borderline)
>   - Definition: Incremental contributions with possible long-term benefits, not immediately transformative.
> 
> - Novelty 3-4 (Tangential)
>   - Definition: Minor or domain-specific improvements with limited broader impact.
> 
> - Novelty 1-2 (Low)
>   - Definition: Methods/application not applicable to core topics.
> 
> 
> ## Papers
> 
> [PAPER LIST HERE]
> 
> ## Relevant Topics
> 
> Use the following relevance criteria when considering which papers to keep. To be rated as highly relevant, a paper must fit at least one of the relevant topics below. Papers which fit multiple categories should be rated more highly.
> 
> 1. Near-Field Cosmology
>    - Relevant: Astrophysics work focusing on dwarf satellite galaxies of the Milky Way, extragalactic satellite luminosity functions, measurements of dark matter density profiles (core-cusp), abundances of dwarf galaxies, signs of historic interactions between the Milky Way and other galaxies (e.g., the accreted stellar halo). Both theoretical and observational work is relevant.
>    - Irrelevant: Papers that focus on Milky Way properties like the stellar disk, bar, and Galactic center. Papers that focus on higher mass galaxies, like massive spirals and elliptical galaxies.
> 
> 2. Resolved Star Formation Histories
>    - Relevant: Observational work deriving star formation histories from observed color-magnitude diagrams of galaxies. Theoretical work measuring star formation histories of simulated galaxies is also relevant, but should be weighted lower.
> 
> 3. Star Formation Quenching
>    - Relevant: Observational work discussing the quenching of star formation in galaxies, **particularly dwarf galaxies**. Work on higher mass galaxies may still be relevant, but should be weighted lower. Theoretical work on the quenching of star formation of dwarf galaxies is also relevant.
> 
> 4. Stellar Models
>    - Relevant: Theoretical work presenting new stellar models, either stellar evolutionary tracks (i.e., stellar interior models) or stellar atmosphere models. Work presenting new tables of bolometric corrections is also relevant.
> 
> 5. Numerical Galaxy Simulations
>    - Relevant: Theoretical work presenting new numerical simulations of galaxies is relevant, especially high-resolution simulations. Simulations that include new physics models (e.g., ionizing radiation, cosmic ray transport) should be given increased weight. Work analyzing previously published simulations should be weighted lower, unless the work also pertains to one of the other relevant topics. Semi-analytic work on galaxies that matches the other criteria is also relevant.
>    - Irrelevant: Numerical simulations that do not focus on galaxies and do not fulfill other criteria are not relevant. Examples include simulations of tidal disruption events, black hole accretion disks, and planet formation.
> 
> 6. Star Formation Driven Outflows
>    - Relevant: Observational and theoretical work examining the role the star formation processes play on driving galaxy-scale gas outflows. Measurements of mass-loading factors are particularly relevant. Work commenting on the phase structure of the outflows is also relevant. Work on galaxies more massive than the LMC should be weighted lower.
>    - Irrelevant: Papers that focus on outflows driven by sources other than stellar feedback, such as AGN-driven feedback. Papers are relevant if they include discussion of both star-formation-driven outflows and other types of outflows.
> 
> 7. Circumgalactic Medium
>    - Relevant: Observational and theoretical work examining the circumgalactic media (CGM) of dwarf galaxies is relevant. Increased weight should be given to work that connects the CGM to ISM outflows from the galaxy.
> 
> 
> 
> **Keywords:**
> 
> - Relevant: Near-field cosmology, satellite luminosity function, core-cusp, substructure, resolved star formation history, satellite quenching, mass-loading factor
> - Irrelevant: bar, Galactic center, tidal disruption event (TDE), supermassive black hole (SMBH)
